# Computational Thresholds for Balanced and Fixed-Slice Independent Sets in Bipartite Graphs

**arXiv ID**: 2608.02503v1
**Authors**: Ijay Narang, Will Perkins, Yuzhou Wang, Timothy L. H. Wee
**Published**: 2026-08-03
**Categories**: cs.DS, cs.CC
**HTML URL**: https://arxiv.org/html/2608.02503v1

## Abstract

Motivated by recent work of Kocurek, Oveis Gharan, and Tjowasi, which gives an efficient sampling algorithm for the hard-core model on random regular bipartite graphs by decomposing into fixed-size slices, we study the worst-case tractability of approximate counting and sampling of fixed-size slices for bipartite independent set problems. Let $G=(L\sqcup R,E)$ be a bipartite graph with $|L|=|R|=n$ and maximum degree $Δ$. The fixed-slice problem asks to sample uniformly from independent sets satisfying $|I\cap L|=α_L n$ and $|I\cap R|=α_R n$. We show that if the overall density $α$ lies in the interval $(\frac{1}Δ, \tfrac{1}{2})$, and the densities on the two sides are more balanced than the typical phase densities of a random $Δ$-regular bipartite graph, then there is no FPRAS or efficient sampling scheme unless $\mathbf{NP}=\mathbf{RP}$.   We then study a related fugacity model in which the densities are not fixed, but the independent set is required to be balanced between the two sides of the bipartition. For $λ>0$, the balanced hard-core model is the ordinary hard-core model with fugacity $λ$, conditioned on the event $|I\cap L|=|I\cap R|$. We prove that this model has the same computational threshold as the hard-core model on general bounded-degree graphs. That is, for every fixed $Δ\ge 3$, if $λ<λ_c(Δ)$, then the balanced partition function admits an FPTAS and the balanced hard-core distribution admits an efficient sampling scheme. Conversely, if $λ>λ_c(Δ)$, then no FPRAS or efficient sampler exists on this graph class unless $\mathbf{NP}=\mathbf{RP}$.

## Computational Thresholds for Balanced and Fixed-Slice Independent Sets in Bipartite GraphsIjay NarangSchool of Computer Science, Georgia Institute of Technology, inarang3@gatech.eduWill PerkinsSchool of Computer Science, Georgia Institute of Technology, wperkins3@gatech.eduYuzhou WangSchool of Mathematics, Georgia Institute of Technology, ywang3694@gatech.eduTimothy L. H. WeeSchool of Mathematics, Georgia Institute of Technology, timothy.wee@gatech.edu

## Abstract

Motivated by recent work of Kocurek, Oveis Gharan, and Tjowasi[28], which gives an efficient sampling algorithm for the hard-core model on random
regular bipartite graphs by decomposing into fixed-size slices, we study the worst-case tractability of approximate counting and sampling of fixed-size slices for bipartite independent set problems.
LetG=(L⊔R,E)G=(L\sqcup R,E)be a bipartite graph with|L|=|R|=n|L|=|R|=nand maximum degreeΔ\Delta. The fixed-slice problem asks to sample uniformly from independent sets satisfying|I∩L|=αL​n|I\cap L|=\alpha_{L}nand|I∩R|=αR​n|I\cap R|=\alpha_{R}n. We show that if the overall densityα\alphalies in the interval(1Δ,12)(\frac{1}{\Delta},\tfrac{1}{2}), and the densities on the two sides are more balanced than the typical phase densities of a randomΔ\Delta-regular bipartite graph, then there is no FPRAS or efficient sampling scheme unless𝐍𝐏=𝐑𝐏\mathbf{NP}=\mathbf{RP}.

We then study a related fugacity model in which the densities are not fixed,
but the independent set is required to be balanced between the two sides of the bipartition. Forλ>0\lambda>0, the balanced
hard-core model is the ordinary hard-core model with fugacityλ\lambda,
conditioned on the event|I∩L|=|I∩R||I\cap L|=|I\cap R|. We prove that this model has
the same computational threshold as the hard-core model on general
bounded-degree graphs. That is, for every fixedΔ≥3\Delta\geq 3, ifλ<λc​(Δ)\lambda<\lambda_{c}(\Delta), then the balanced partition function admits an
FPTAS and the balanced hard-core distribution admits an efficient sampling
scheme. Conversely, ifλ>λc​(Δ)\lambda>\lambda_{c}(\Delta), then no FPRAS or efficient sampler exists on this graph class unless𝐍𝐏=𝐑𝐏\mathbf{NP}=\mathbf{RP}.

## 1Introduction

The hard-core model originates from the study of lattice gas systems in statistical physics and has received much attention in theoretical computer science and related fields. Given a graphGGand fugacityλ>0\lambda>0, the partition function of the hard-core model is given byZG​(λ):=∑I∈ℐGλ|I|,Z_{G}(\lambda):=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{G}}\lambda^{|I|},

whereℐG\mathcal{I}_{G}is the set of all independent sets ofGG. Whenλ=1\lambda=1, the partition function equals|ℐG||\mathcal{I}_{G}|, the number of independent sets ofGG. Associated toZG​(λ)Z_{G}(\lambda)is the probability measure onℐG\mathcal{I}_{G}given byμG,λ​(I)=λ|I|ZG​(λ).\mu_{G,\lambda}(I)=\frac{\lambda^{|I|}}{Z_{G}(\lambda)}.

Algorithmically, there are two main tasks associated with the hard-core model. The first is to compute (exactly or approximately)ZG​(λ)Z_{G}(\lambda); the second is to output an independent set with distribution (close to)μG,λ\mu_{G,\lambda}. Even in restricted settings (including bounded-degree or bipartite graphs) computingZG​(λ)Z_{G}(\lambda)is#​P\#\textbf{P}-hard and so most attention is focused on when it is possible to efficiently computeZG​(λ)Z_{G}(\lambda)to within anϵ\epsilonrelative error.

More precisely, we collect the following definitions, which will be used throughout the paper. Afully polynomial-time approximation scheme(FPTAS) is a deterministic algorithm that givenGGandϵ∈(0,1)\epsilon\in(0,1)outputsZ^\widehat{Z}satisfying(1−ϵ)​ZG​(λ)≤Z^≤(1+ϵ)​ZG​(λ)(1-\epsilon)Z_{G}(\lambda)\leq\widehat{Z}\leq(1+\epsilon)Z_{G}(\lambda)

in time polynomial in|V​(G)||V(G)|and1/ϵ1/\epsilon. Afully polynomial-time randomized approximation scheme(FPRAS) is a randomized algorithm that outputs such an approximation with probability at least2/32/3and runs in time polynomial in|V​(G)||V(G)|and1/ϵ1/\epsilon. Anefficient sampling scheme, in our context, is a randomized algorithm that runs in time polynomial in|V​(G)||V(G)|and1/ϵ1/\epsilon, and outputs an independent set according to a distributionμ^\hat{\mu}satisfying‖μG,λ−μ^‖TV<ϵ.\|\mu_{G,\lambda}-\hat{\mu}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}<\epsilon.

Let𝒢Δ\mathcal{G}_{\Delta}be the set of all graphs of maximum degree at mostΔ\Delta. ForG∈𝒢ΔG\in\mathcal{G}_{\Delta}, the computational tractability of approximatingZG​(λ)Z_{G}(\lambda)is well understood. Ifλ<λc​(Δ)\lambda<\lambda_{c}(\Delta), then there exists an FPTAS and efficient sampling scheme for the hard-core model[38]. On the other hand, ifλ>λc​(Δ)\lambda>\lambda_{c}(\Delta), then there is no FPRAS or efficient sampling scheme unless𝐍𝐏=𝐑𝐏\mathbf{NP}=\mathbf{RP}[36,37,19]. Hereλc​(Δ):=(Δ−1)Δ−1(Δ−2)Δ\lambda_{c}(\Delta):=\frac{(\Delta-1)^{\Delta-1}}{(\Delta-2)^{\Delta}}is known as the uniqueness threshold, as it
corresponds to the boundary between uniqueness and non-uniqueness of the infinite volume hard-core Gibbs measure on theΔ\Delta-regular tree (see, e.g.[21]).

WhenGGis restricted to be bipartite, however, proving hardness becomes substantially more delicate, since theNP-hard optimization problems used in hardness reductions become tractable on bipartite graphs. This difficulty is reflected in the status of approximately counting independent sets in bipartite graphs, known as#​BIS\#\textbf{BIS}, which is one of the central problems in approximate counting. It is neither known to admit an FPRAS nor known to beNP-hard to approximate[13,14].

For bipartiteG∈𝒢ΔG\in\mathcal{G}_{\Delta},[3]showed that approximatingZG​(λ)Z_{G}(\lambda)is as hard as#​BIS\#\textbf{BIS}wheneverλ>λc​(Δ)\lambda>\lambda_{c}(\Delta). The algorithmic side has seen some progress for special classes of instances including bipartite graphs with degree bounds on one side[31,8]and lattices[22,4]and expander graphs[25,30,27,26]for large values ofλ\lambda.

Additionally, randomΔ\Delta-regular bipartite graphs are canonical average-case instances. Combined with the algorithmic results forλ=Ω​(log⁡Δ/Δ)\lambda=\Omega(\log\Delta/\Delta)[10], following[25,30], the recent work[28]giving efficient algorithms forλ=O​(Δ−1/2)\lambda=O(\Delta^{-1/2}),
shows that, for randomΔ\Delta-regular bipartite graphs (forΔ\Deltaa sufficiently large constant), one can efficiently
sample from the hard-core model for all fugacities. A key feature of the
algorithm and analysis of[28]is the use of fixed-size slices. Instead of directly sampling from the hard-core measureμG,λ\mu_{G,\lambda}, they sample from both the one-sided and two-sided slices of bipartite independent sets; that is, the hard-core measure conditioned on the number of left-occupied vertices beingkk(the one-sided slice) and the hard-core measure conditioned on the number of left-occupied vertices beingkLk_{L}and the number of right-occupied vertices beingkRk_{R}.

For a bipartite graphG=(L⊔R,E)G=(L\sqcup R,E)with|L|=|R|=n|L|=|R|=nand densities(αL,αR)∈[0,1]2(\alpha_{L},\alpha_{R})\in[0,1]^{2}, a fixed slice consists of the independent setsℐαL,αR​(G):={I∈ℐG:|I∩L|=⌊αL​n⌋,|I∩R|=⌊αR​n⌋}.\mathcal{I}_{\alpha_{L},\alpha_{R}}(G):=\{I\in\mathcal{I}_{G}:|I\cap L|=\lfloor\alpha_{L}n\rfloor,\hskip 2.84544pt|I\cap R|=\lfloor\alpha_{R}n\rfloor\}.

We writeFixedSlice​(αL,αR)\mathrm{FixedSlice}(\alpha_{L},\alpha_{R})for the associated problems of approximately countingZGslice​(αL,αR):=|ℐαL,αR|Z^{\mathrm{slice}}_{G}(\alpha_{L},\alpha_{R}):=|\mathcal{I}_{\alpha_{L},\alpha_{R}}|and approximately sampling fromμG,αL,αRslice\mu^{\mathrm{slice}}_{G,\alpha_{L},\alpha_{R}}, the uniform distribution onℐαL,αR\mathcal{I}_{\alpha_{L},\alpha_{R}}. The algorithmic analysis of[28]proceeds by proving the spectral independence
estimates needed to obtain rapid mixing on the fixed(αL,αR)(\alpha_{L},\alpha_{R})-slices used to sample from the hard-core model.

Thus, one may hope that sampling via fixed slices could lead to progress on#​BIS\#\textbf{BIS}. Our first main result shows that this approach fails in the
worst case: there exist fixed bipartite instances for which the corresponding fixed-slice problem isNP-hard.

To state the main result, we need some notation related to densities. Forλ>λc​(Δ)\lambda>\lambda_{c}(\Delta), the hard-core model on the infiniteΔ\Delta-regular tree exhibits non-uniqueness. Two distinct semi-translation-invariant Gibbs measures are obtained by taking the limit of finite Gibbs measures with even-occupied and odd-occupied boundary conditions respectively. We writeα+​(λ)\alpha_{+}(\lambda)andα−​(λ)\alpha_{-}(\lambda)for the probability the root of the tree is occupied under these respective measures. Whenλ≤λc​(Δ)\lambda\leq\lambda_{c}(\Delta)we haveα+​(λ)=α−​(λ)\alpha_{+}(\lambda)=\alpha_{-}(\lambda)since there is a unique infinite-volume Gibbs measure; whenλ>λc​(Δ)\lambda>\lambda_{c}(\Delta), we haveα+​(λ)>α−​(λ)\alpha_{+}(\lambda)>\alpha_{-}(\lambda). We defineα​(λ):=(α+​(λ)+α−​(λ))/2\alpha(\lambda):=(\alpha_{+}(\lambda)+\alpha_{-}(\lambda))/2. This is also the limiting expected density of the hard-core model on the randomΔ\Delta-regular bipartite graph[37]. Next we letαc​(Δ):=α​(λc​(Δ))=1Δ\alpha_{c}(\Delta):=\alpha(\lambda_{c}(\Delta))=\frac{1}{\Delta}. Finally note that the mapλ∈(λc,∞)↦α​(λ)∈(1/Δ,1/2)\lambda\in(\lambda_{c},\infty)\mapsto\alpha(\lambda)\in(1/\Delta,1/2)is strictly increasing, and hence invertible on the relevant range. We denote its inverse byλ​(α)\lambda(\alpha).

Let𝒢Δ,Δ\mathcal{G}_{\Delta,\Delta}denote the class of bipartite graphsG=(L⊔R,E)G=(L\sqcup R,E)with maximum degree at mostΔ\Deltaand|L|=|R||L|=|R|.

## Theorem 1.1.

Fix an integerΔ≥3\Delta\geq 3andαL,αR∈(0,1)\alpha_{L},\alpha_{R}\in(0,1). Letα=(αL+αR)/2\alpha=(\alpha_{L}+\alpha_{R})/2. Ifα∈(αc​(Δ),1/2)\alpha\in(\alpha_{c}(\Delta),1/2)andα−​(λ​(α))α+​(λ​(α))<αLαR<α+​(λ​(α))α−​(λ​(α)),\frac{\alpha_{-}(\lambda(\alpha))}{\alpha_{+}(\lambda(\alpha))}<\frac{\alpha_{L}}{\alpha_{R}}<\frac{\alpha_{+}(\lambda(\alpha))}{\alpha_{-}(\lambda(\alpha))},(1.1)

then, unless𝐍𝐏=𝐑𝐏\mathbf{NP}=\mathbf{RP}, there is no FPRAS or efficient sampling scheme forFixedSlice​(αL,αR)\mathrm{FixedSlice}(\alpha_{L},\alpha_{R})for inputsG∈𝒢Δ,ΔG\in\mathcal{G}_{\Delta,\Delta}.

Thus, fixed-slice decompositions do not by themselves alleviate worst-case
hardness in bipartite graphs: some fixed slices are alreadyNP-hard to approximately count and sample. The hard
slices in our theorem are necessarily off the phase-aligned ratios that are
typical under the relevant hard-core Gibbs measure on random bipartite graphs, so this result does not
rule out slice-based algorithms for#​BIS\#\textbf{BIS}. Rather, it identifies a
worst-case obstruction and motivates the question of which slices are
tractable. We discuss the relationship between different regimes of fixed slices and how they relate to our hardness results and the work of[28]in Section1.2.

The fixed-slice model fixes both coordinates(|I∩L|,|I∩R|)(|I\cap L|,|I\cap R|).
We next consider a softer but still global constraint, in which only their
differenceB​(I):=|I∩L|−|I∩R|B(I):=|I\cap L|-|I\cap R|is fixed. A natural such constraint isbalance: we require the independent set to use the two
sides equally. Indeed, balance constraints introduce a related source of hardness on bipartite graphs. Although the maximum independent set problem is polynomial-time solvable on bipartite graphs, maximum balanced independent set isNP-hard[15]. Moreover,[34]showed that, in random bipartite graphs, maximum balanced independent set exhibits the same kind of statistical–computational gap as maximum independent set in random graphs, namely hardness for local and low-degree algorithms.

LetℐGbal\mathcal{I}_{G}^{\mathrm{bal}}denote the collection of balanced independent sets ofGG; that isℐGbal={I∈ℐG:|I∩L|=|I∩R|}\mathcal{I}_{G}^{\mathrm{bal}}=\{I\in\mathcal{I}_{G}:|I\cap L|=|I\cap R|\}.
Forλ>0\lambda>0, define the balanced partition function and the associated Gibbs distribution byZGbal​(λ)\displaystyle Z_{G}^{\mathrm{bal}}(\lambda)=∑I∈ℐGbalλ|I|,\displaystyle=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{G}^{\mathrm{bal}}}\lambda^{|I|},μG,λbal​(I)\displaystyle\mu_{G,\lambda}^{\mathrm{bal}}(I)=λ|I|ZGbal​(λ),I∈ℐGbal.\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda^{|I|}}{Z_{G}^{\mathrm{bal}}(\lambda)},\qquad I\in\mathcal{I}_{G}^{\mathrm{bal}}.

Given this model, a natural question is whether approximatingZGbal​(λ)Z_{G}^{\mathrm{bal}}(\lambda)forG∈𝒢Δ,ΔG\in\mathcal{G}_{\Delta,\Delta}exhibits the same hardness threshold as approximating the hard-core
partition function on general bounded-degree graphs. We answer this
question affirmatively by proving the following theorem.

## Theorem 1.2.

Fix an integerΔ≥3\Delta\geq 3and a fugacityλ>0\lambda>0. Then the following hold.
- •

Ifλ<λc​(Δ),\lambda<\lambda_{c}(\Delta),there is an FPTAS forZGbal​(λ)Z_{G}^{\mathrm{bal}}(\lambda)and an efficient sampling scheme forμG,λbal\mu_{G,\lambda}^{\mathrm{bal}}on inputsG∈𝒢Δ,ΔG\in\mathcal{G}_{\Delta,\Delta}.
- •

Ifλ>λc​(Δ)\lambda>\lambda_{c}(\Delta), then unless𝐍𝐏=𝐑𝐏\mathbf{NP}=\mathbf{RP}, there is no FPRAS forZGbal​(λ)Z_{G}^{\mathrm{bal}}(\lambda)or efficient sampling scheme forμG,λbal\mu_{G,\lambda}^{\mathrm{bal}}on inputsG∈𝒢Δ,ΔG\in\mathcal{G}_{\Delta,\Delta}.

For the positive algorithmic result, we in fact treat a larger class of graphs than𝒢Δ,Δ\mathcal{G}_{\Delta,\Delta}: bipartite graphs of maximum degreeΔ\Deltafor which the ratio|L|/|R||L|/|R|is not too far from11; see (2.1) below.
We do not address the critical caseλ=λc​(Δ)\lambda=\lambda_{c}(\Delta), but results for the hard-core model[7]suggest that this case should be tractable.

The challenge to proving the positive algorithm result of Theorem1.2is that the event|I∩L|=|I∩R||I\cap L|=|I\cap R|can be atypical and exponentially rare under the unconstrained hard-core measure, so naive rejection sampling may fail to be efficient. The main idea is to introduce an exponential tiltet​B​(I)e^{tB(I)}that makes the exact balanced event more typical. At a nearly centering tilt, balance has probability of ordern−1/2n^{-1/2}, making rejection sampling plausible. This exponential tilt, however, takes us away from the usual hard-core model to a model with different fugacities for vertices inLLandRR, and the main part of the analysis is showing that we can sample efficiently from this measure.

Aboveλc​(Δ)\lambda_{c}(\Delta), the same global constraint becomes a source of hardness. We build upon the phase coexistence gadget framework of Sly[36]and extensions to models with global constraints in[11,6]. The hard-core model on such a gadget (derived from random bipartite graphs) displays two phases: one in which the left side is more heavily occupied, and one in which the right side is more heavily occupied. By replacing each vertex with a gadget, the phase of each gadget can be used as a binary label. Exact balance then forces these labels to appear in equal numbers, while the edges between gadgets reward labelings with few crossing edges. This allows the balanced partition function to encode the bisection problem.

Previous work on the worst-case complexity of approximate counting and sampling under global constraints include[11,23]on sampling independent sets of specified size on bounded degree graphs and[6,29]on sampling from the ferromagnetic Ising model at fixed magnetization on bounded degree graphs.

## 1.1Overview of the techniques

## 1.1.1Algorithms below uniqueness

We fixλ<λc​(Δ)\lambda<\lambda_{c}(\Delta)and consider the balanced hard-core modelμG,λbal\mu_{G,\lambda}^{\text{bal}}onG∈𝒢Δ,ΔG\in\mathcal{G}_{\Delta,\Delta}. This model is equivalent to the usual hard-core model conditioned on thebalanceB​(I)B(I)of an independent set being zero, whereB​(I):=|I∩L|−|I∩R|.B(I):=|I\cap L|-|I\cap R|.(1.2)

Our strategy revolves around a relaxed version of this balanced model: atiltedhard-core model with partition functionZG​(λ;t):=∑I∈ℐGλ|I|​et​B​(I)=∑I∈ℐG(λ​et)|I∩L|​(λ​e−t)|I∩R|,\displaystyle Z_{G}(\lambda;t):=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{G}}\lambda^{\left|I\right|}e^{tB(I)}=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{G}}\left(\lambda e^{t}\right)^{\left|I\cap L\right|}\left(\lambda e^{-t}\right)^{\left|I\cap R\right|},(1.3)

which is a bivariate hard-core model with fugacitiesλ​et\lambda e^{t}andλ​e−t\lambda e^{-t}onLLandRRrespectively. WriteμG,λ,t\mu_{G,\lambda,t}for the tilted measure associated toZG​(λ;t)Z_{G}(\lambda;t). The link is thatet​B​(I)=1e^{tB(I)}=1wheneverB​(I)=0B(I)=0, so the conditional law ofμG,λ,t\mu_{G,\lambda,t}givenB​(I)=0B(I)=0is exactlyμG,λbal\mu_{G,\lambda}^{\text{bal}}.

The tiltttgives us flexibility to favor occupation inLLandRRby settingttto be positive or negative respectively. A monotonicity argument shows that there is a uniquet∗t^{*}for which𝔼μG,λ,t∗​B​(I)=0\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{G,\lambda,t^{*}}}B(I)=0.

Our sampling algorithm is relatively straightforward. At a high level, the steps are:
- (i)

We first perform noisy binary search by empirical sampling from the tilted measure to find at~\widetilde{t}that approximatest∗t^{*}, in the sense|𝔼μG,λ,t~​B​(I)|=O​(1)|\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{G,\lambda,\widetilde{t}}}B(I)|=O(1).
- (ii)

Then we rejection sample fromμG,λ,t~\mu_{G,\lambda,\widetilde{t}}until we obtain an independent setIIwithB​(I)=0B(I)=0.

The proof that this sampling scheme forμG,λbal\mu_{G,\lambda}^{\text{bal}}can be made efficient uses several technical ingredients. We show that sampling fromμG,λ,t\mu_{G,\lambda,t}can be done efficiently by a self-avoiding walk (SAW) tree approach building upon[38]. We defer technical definitions of the SAW tree and its boundary conditions to Section2.2. Nevertheless, we highlight informally a key step which is to establish a form of correlation decay,strong spatial mixing(SSM), on the SAW tree.

For a boundary conditionτ\tauon the SAW tree rooted at vertexvv, letRvτR_{v}^{\tau}denote the resulting root occupation ratio, i.e. the ratio of the probabilities that the root is occupied to unoccupied.

## Theorem(Informal version of Theorem2.8).

Fixλ<λc​(Δ)\lambda<\lambda_{c}(\Delta). For every fixedtt, the tilted hard-core model (1.3) satisfies SSM on the SAW tree. That is, if two boundary conditionsτ,τ′\tau,\tau^{\prime}first differ at distanceℓ\ellfrom the root, then|Rvτ−Rvτ′|≤C​e−c​ℓ,|R_{v}^{\tau}-R_{v}^{\tau^{\prime}}|\leq Ce^{-c\ell},

whereC,c>0C,c>0depend only onΔ,λ,t\Delta,\lambda,t.

In particular, ifttis restricted to a fixed compact interval, the constantsC,cC,cmay be chosen uniformly over all graphs, roots, and boundary conditions. Consequently, truncating the SAW tree at logarithmic depth gives a polynomial-time oracle for the marginal occupation probabilities in the tilted model. A standard self-reduction argument then yields an efficient sampler forμG,λ,t\mu_{G,\lambda,t}.

The argument is rather delicate and standard estimates do not apply: while the base fugacityλ\lambdais belowλc​(Δ)\lambda_{c}(\Delta), one of the tilted fugacitiesλ​et\lambda e^{t}andλ​e−t\lambda e^{-t}may rise above this threshold. We instead exploit the bipartite structure and show that the occupation-ratio recursion on the SAW tree is contractive in a two-level sense, corresponding to a recursion onGGthat maps fromL→R→LL\to R\to Lor vice versa. We show this contraction after passing to the coordinate system induced by the potential functionx↦arcsinh⁡xx\mapsto\operatorname{arcsinh}\sqrt{x}(also used in[35,9]).

ForG∈𝒢Δ,ΔG\in\mathcal{G}_{\Delta,\Delta}, the centering tiltt∗t^{*}can indeed be shown to live in a compact interval that does not scale with|V​(G)|\left|V(G)\right|. In addition to being essential for the SSM arguments, this compactness allows us to establish a zero-freeness result for a complex version ofZG​(λ;t)Z_{G}(\lambda;t)in some region around the real line. The latter leads to a local central limit theorem forB​(I)B(I), which in turn gives the acceptance probability estimatePrμG,λ,t~⁡{B​(I)=0}=Ω​(|V​(G)|−1/2).\Pr_{\mu_{G,\lambda,\widetilde{t}}}\{B(I)=0\}=\Omega\!\left(|V(G)|^{-1/2}\right).

This estimate provides the guarantee that rejection sampling succeeds in polynomial time.

We now turn to the FPTAS forZGbal​(λ)Z_{G}^{\text{bal}}(\lambda). The tilted hard-core model plays a key role again, along with the identityZGbal​(λ)=ZG​(λ;t)​PrμG,λ,t⁡{B​(I)=0}.Z_{G}^{\mathrm{bal}}(\lambda)=Z_{G}(\lambda;t)\Pr_{\mu_{G,\lambda,t}}\{B(I)=0\}.

The three tasks are thus (1) to find a nearly centered tiltt~\widetilde{t}so that the probability factor is inverse-polynomially large, (2) to give an FPTAS forZG​(λ;t~)Z_{G}(\lambda;\widetilde{t}), and (3) to approximate this probability factor efficiently.

In task (1) we replace the noisy binary search used in the sampling procedure with a more complicated deterministic procedure, based on deterministic estimates of𝔼μG,λ,t⁡B​(I)\operatorname{\mathbbm{E}}_{\mu_{G,\lambda,t}}B(I). Tasks (2) and (3) essentially recycle the ingredients from the sampling proof, with task (3) “algorithmizing” the local CLT proof, in the spirit of[24].

## Remark 1.3.

Chen–Liu–Yin[8]study bivariate hard-core models on bipartite graphs related to the tilted modelZG​(λ;t)Z_{G}(\lambda;t). They allow for a larger class of bipartite graphs requiring only a degree bound on one side. They give a uniqueness condition phrased in terms of the fixed points of a mapx↦λL​(1+λR​(1+x)−w)−(Δ−1)x\mapsto\lambda_{L}\left(1+\lambda_{R}(1+x)^{-w}\right)^{-(\Delta-1)}, where(λL,λR)(\lambda_{L},\lambda_{R})are the left and right fugacities, and wherew>0w>0is a branching parameter for the (possibly) unbounded degree side. Under this condition, they prove spectral independence, influence decay, and rapid mixing.

Our tilted measure analysis pertains to a subfamily of such models, where(λL,λR)=(λ​et,λ​e−t)(\lambda_{L},\lambda_{R})=(\lambda e^{t},\lambda e^{-t})traces a curveλL​λR=λ2\lambda_{L}\lambda_{R}=\lambda^{2}in the bivariate fugacity space. Here we prove auxiliary SSM results for the associated SAW tree, zero-freeness for the tilted partition functionZG​(λ;t)Z_{G}(\lambda;t), and local CLT results for the balance variableB​(I)B(I), in order to handle the exact balance modelZGbal​(λ)Z^{\text{bal}}_{G}(\lambda). Furthermore, our analysis is tight, as witnessed byλL=λR=λc​(Δ)\lambda_{L}=\lambda_{R}=\lambda_{c}(\Delta).

## 1.1.2Hardness above uniqueness

Above the uniqueness threshold, both hardness results are proved by adapting the phase-coexistence gadget framework of[36,6].
Briefly, the gadget is obtained from a random bipartiteΔ\Delta-regular graph by a modification that creates designated terminal vertices. In the non-uniqueness regimeλ>λc​(Δ)\lambda>\lambda_{c}(\Delta), the hard-core measure on this gadget has two dominant phases: in the(+)(+)-phase the left side is more heavily occupied, while in the(−)(-)-phase the right side is more heavily occupied.
Given an input graphHH, we replace each vertexx∈V​(H)x\in V(H)by a gadget copyGxG_{x}and connect the terminals of different copies according to the edges ofHH. The phase vectorY=(Yx)x∈V​(H)∈{+,−}V​(H)Y=(Y_{x})_{x\in V(H)}\in\{+,-\}^{V(H)}then plays the role of a spin configuration onHH.

For the balanced model, the global constraint|I∩L|=|I∩R||I\cap L|=|I\cap R|forces the phase vector to be nearly balanced. Informally, before the inter-gadget edges are imposed, phase vectors with unequal numbers of(+)(+)-phase and(−)(-)-phase gadgets have exponentially small contribution after conditioning on exact balance, while balanced phase vectors retain enough mass to dominate the constrained partition function. Thus the exact balance constraint converts the possible phase vectors into bisections of the input graph.

The inter-gadget edges then encode the cut objective.
Terminal occupations are approximately independent under the phase-conditioned gadget measures, so the probability that all inter-gadget edges are legal factors over the edges ofHH.
With our choice of terminal matchings, an edge whose endpoints have the same phase contributes a larger factor than an edge whose endpoints have opposite phases.
Hence, among balanced phase vectors, the dominant contribution to the balanced partition function comes from phase vectors minimizing the number of cut edges.
This allows us to recover the minimum bisection value from sufficiently accurate multiplicative approximations to the balanced partition function.

The fixed-slice hardness proof reuses the same construction and terminal compatibility calculation.
The difference is that the constraint now fixes the two side occupations separately, rather than only their difference.
We choose the gadget fugacity and the number of isolated vertices so that the target slice is centered around phase vectors with a prescribed number of plus phases.
The analogue of the balance point probability estimate is now a two-dimensional point probability estimate for(|I∩L|,|I∩R|)(|I\cap L|,|I\cap R|).
Phase vectors with the wrong number of plus phases are exponentially suppressed, while correctly centered phase vectors retain enough mass.
The same inter-gadget compatibility factor then encodes the corresponding fixed-cardinality cut problem.

## 1.2Fixed-Size Sampling and Future Directions

Schematically, the decomposition used in[28]can be viewed asZG​(λ)\displaystyle Z_{G}(\lambda)≈∑I∈ℐG:|I∩X|≤α0​|X|,|I∩Y|≤α0​|Y|λ|I|+∑α0​|X|<k≤β0​|X|∑S⊆X:|S|=kλk​(1+λ)|Y∖N​(S)|\displaystyle\approx\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\in\mathcal{I}_{G}:\\
|I\cap X|\leq\alpha_{0}|X|,\ |I\cap Y|\leq\alpha_{0}|Y|\end{subarray}}\lambda^{|I|}+\sum_{\alpha_{0}|X|<k\leq\beta_{0}|X|}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subseteq X:\\
|S|=k\end{subarray}}\lambda^{k}(1+\lambda)^{|Y\setminus N(S)|}+∑α0​|Y|<k≤β0​|Y|∑S⊆Y:|S|=kλk​(1+λ)|X∖N​(S)|\displaystyle+\sum_{\alpha_{0}|Y|<k\leq\beta_{0}|Y|}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subseteq Y:\\
|S|=k\end{subarray}}\lambda^{k}(1+\lambda)^{|X\setminus N(S)|}(1.4)

whereα0:=log⁡Δ(2+oΔ​(1))​Δ,β0:=Θ​(λ)\alpha_{0}:=\frac{\log\Delta}{(2+o_{\Delta}(1))\Delta},\qquad\beta_{0}:=\Theta(\lambda)

The first sum (low-low) is approximated by sampling from each fixed two-sided slice via a down-up walk. The second and third sums are approximated by running a different down-up walk on one-sided slices. WhenGGis a randomΔ\Delta-regular graph, their analysis shows that these walks mix in polynomial time.

Now, consider the hardness result of Theorem1.1. In particular, for each fixed0<ϵ<10<\epsilon<1, the low-low slices(ρL,ρR)=((1−ϵ)​ρ,(1+ϵ)​ρ)(\rho_{L},\rho_{R})=((1-\epsilon)\rho,(1+\epsilon)\rho)are hard in the worst-case by Theorem1.1forρϵ​(Δ)<ρ≤α0/(1+ϵ)\rho_{\epsilon}(\Delta)<\rho\leq\alpha_{0}/(1+\epsilon), whereρϵ​(Δ):=inf{ρ>αc​(Δ):α−​(λ​(ρ))/α+​(λ​(ρ))<(1−ϵ)/(1+ϵ)}\rho_{\epsilon}(\Delta):=\inf\{\rho>\alpha_{c}(\Delta):\alpha_{-}(\lambda(\rho))/\alpha_{+}(\lambda(\rho))<(1-\epsilon)/(1+\epsilon)\}, while phase-aligned slices are not ruled out by our theorem.

It would be interesting to understand exactly which fixed slices are hard. One future direction is an understanding of the complement region. That is the following problem,

## Problem 1.4.

Fix an integerΔ≥3\Delta\geq 3andα∈(αc​(Δ),1/2)\alpha\in(\alpha_{c}(\Delta),1/2). If(αL+αR)/2=α(\alpha_{L}+\alpha_{R})/2=\alphaandαLαR∈(0,α−​(λ​(α))α+​(λ​(α))]∪[α+​(λ​(α))α−​(λ​(α)),∞),\frac{\alpha_{L}}{\alpha_{R}}\in\bigg(0,\frac{\alpha_{-}(\lambda(\alpha))}{\alpha_{+}(\lambda(\alpha))}\bigg]\cup\bigg[\frac{\alpha_{+}(\lambda(\alpha))}{\alpha_{-}(\lambda(\alpha))},\infty\bigg),(1.5)

doesℐαL,αR\mathcal{I}_{\alpha_{L},\alpha_{R}}admit an FPRAS and efficient sampling scheme?

In particular, an answer to Problem1.4would give an explicit boundary for the computability of the fixed-slice problem above the uniqueness threshold. Currently, it is unclear if the boundary is the true threshold for computability or an artifact of our proof.

Lastly, we remark that the comparison between Theorem1.1and the algorithm of[28]should be understood with the quantifiers in mind. The fixed-slice algorithmic results of[28]are proved in the random regular setting and in the large-Δ\Deltaregime, whereas Theorem1.1is a worst-case hardness result valid for every fixedΔ≥3\Delta\geq 3.

## 1.3Organization

The rest of the paper is organized as follows. In Section2, we prove correlation decay for the tilted measures, establish zero-freeness and a local central limit theorem for the balance variable, and combine these ingredients to obtain the sampler forμG,λbal\mu^{\text{bal}}_{G,\lambda}below the uniqueness threshold.

In Section3, we prove the FPTAS below the uniqueness threshold. The proof recycles the sampling ingredients, replacing the noisy binary search by deterministic bisection and using Fourier inversion to approximate the probability of exact balance.

In Section4, we prove the hardness result above the uniqueness threshold. We construct the bipartite gadgets, analyze their phase behavior under the balanced constraint, and give the reduction from the minimum bisection problem. Lastly, in Section5, we study the fixed-slice model and prove hardness for approximately counting and sampling fixed slices at prescribed densities above the tree uniqueness density.

At a high level, to prove that Algorithm1fulfills

- 1.

The binary search requires only polynomially many samples and returns a

window. Strong spatial mixing on the SAW tree throughout this window then
yields an efficient sampler for the tilted measure.
- 3.

The rejection step succeeds with polynomially many trials. Concretely,
we show the key acceptance probability estimatePrμλ,t~⁡{B​(I)=0}=Ω​(1n).\Pr_{\mu_{\lambda,\widetilde{t}}}\{B(I)=0\}=\Omega\!\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right).

which yields a local central limit theorem forB​(I)B(I). Consequently,M=Θ​(n​log⁡(1/ϵ))M=\Theta(\sqrt{n}\log(1/\epsilon))rejection trials suffice.

Point 1 is relatively straightforward and is treated in
Section2.1. Points 2 and 3 constitute the main
work and are treated in the subsequent subsections.

## 2.1Preliminaries

empirical means appearing in the search step of Algorithm1.

## 2.2Marginal occupation probabilities via the SAW tree

We begin by showing that there exists a poly-time algorithm that can approximate the marginal occupation probabilities ofμλ,t\mu_{\lambda,t}within additive error±ϵn\pm\frac{\epsilon}{n}. By standard self-reducibility arguments (see e.g. Weitz[38, Section 5]; cf. Algorithm3and the discussion following it), this will yield anϵ\epsilon-approximate poly-time sampler. Our algorithm for approximating the marginals will be via the self-avoiding walk (SAW) tree[38].

In what follows, we write𝝀=(λu)u∈V\bm{\lambda}=(\lambda_{u})_{u\in V}for a general vector of vertex activities andμG,𝝀\mu_{G,\bm{\lambda}}for the corresponding hard-core measure. The tilted measureμλ,t\mu_{\lambda,t}on bipartiteG=(L⊔R,E)G=(L\sqcup R,E)is the special case obtained by takingλu=λL​(t)\lambda_{u}=\lambda_{L}(t)onLLandλu=λR​(t)\lambda_{u}=\lambda_{R}(t)onRR.

## Definition 2.5(Depth-LLtruncated SAW tree).

Fix a graphG=(V,E)G=(V,E), a rootv∈Vv\in V, vertex activities(λu)u∈V(\lambda_{u})_{u\in V}, and an ordering of the neighbors of each vertex.
The self-avoiding walk treeTSAW​(G,v)T_{\mathrm{SAW}}(G,v)is the tree of all paths originating at rootv=v0v=v_{0}, where each path is built recursively as follows. Given a self-avoiding path(v0,…,vk)(v_{0},\dots,v_{k}), consider the neighborswwofvkv_{k}except the predecessorvk−1v_{k-1}. If there are no neighbors, the path terminates andvkv_{k}is an ordinary leaf. Ifw∉{v0,…,vk}w\notin\left\{v_{0},\dots,v_{k}\right\}, extend the path to(v0,…,vk,w)(v_{0},\dots,v_{k},w). If insteadw=vjw=v_{j}for somej<kj<k, the child is a
(terminal) boundary leaf. This boundary leaf is fixed to be occupied if, in the
ordering atww, the closing edge{vk,w}\{v_{k},w\}is larger than the edge{w,vj+1}\{w,v_{j+1}\}that starts the cycle, and is fixed to be unoccupied
otherwise. Each non-boundary copy of a vertexuuinTSAW​(G,v)T_{\mathrm{SAW}}(G,v)has activityλu\lambda_{u}.

For integerL≥0L\geq 0, the depth-LLtruncationTSAW(L)​(G,v)T_{\mathrm{SAW}}^{(L)}(G,v)is
obtained fromTSAW​(G,v)T_{\mathrm{SAW}}(G,v)by keeping only vertices at distance
at mostLLfrom the root, together with the induced occupied/unoccupied boundary
conditions on any retained boundary leaves.

A boundary conditionτ\tauis an assignment of occupied or unoccupied
states to a subset of vertices ofTSAW​(G,v)T_{\mathrm{SAW}}(G,v)not containing
the root.

LetμTSAW​(G,v),𝝀τ\mu_{T_{\mathrm{SAW}}(G,v),\bm{\lambda}}^{\tau}denote the
hard-core measure onTSAW​(G,v)T_{\mathrm{SAW}}(G,v)conditioned onτ\tau,
together with the occupied/unoccupied boundary conditions on the
cycle-closing leaves specified in the construction of the SAW tree.
Define the root occupation ratio byRvτ:=μTSAW​(G,v),𝝀τ​(σv=1)μTSAW​(G,v),𝝀τ​(σv=0).R_{v}^{\tau}:=\frac{\mu_{T_{\mathrm{SAW}}(G,v),\bm{\lambda}}^{\tau}(\sigma_{v}=1)}{\mu_{T_{\mathrm{SAW}}(G,v),\bm{\lambda}}^{\tau}(\sigma_{v}=0)}.

When no additional boundary conditionτ\tauis imposed, we writeRvR_{v}.
Thus,μTSAW​(G,v),𝝀τ​(σv=1)=Rvτ1+Rvτ.\mu_{T_{\mathrm{SAW}}(G,v),\bm{\lambda}}^{\tau}(\sigma_{v}=1)=\frac{R_{v}^{\tau}}{1+R_{v}^{\tau}}.

By the SAW-tree identity of
Weitz[38, Section 3], the occupation ratioRvR_{v}at the root ofTSAW​(G,v)T_{\mathrm{SAW}}(G,v)equals the occupation ratio atvvinGG, which isμG,𝝀​(σv=1)/μG,𝝀​(σv=0).\mu_{G,\bm{\lambda}}(\sigma_{v}=1)/\mu_{G,\bm{\lambda}}(\sigma_{v}=0).

The truncated SAW tree is used to approximate marginal probabilities in Algorithm2.Algorithm 2Truncated SAW-tree marginal oracle1:A graphG=(V,E)G=(V,E), activities(λu)u∈V(\lambda_{u})_{u\in V},
a vertexv∈Vv\in V, an accuracy parameterϵ>0\epsilon>0.2:An estimatep~v\widetilde{p}_{v}forμG,𝝀​(σv=1)\mu_{G,\bm{\lambda}}(\sigma_{v}=1)3:Choose a truncation depthL≥1c​log⁡(C​nϵ)L\geq\frac{1}{c}\log\!\left(\frac{Cn}{\epsilon}\right)for suitable constantsC,c>0C,c>0and construct the depth-LLtruncated SAW treeTSAW(L)​(G,v)T_{\mathrm{SAW}}^{(L)}(G,v).4:Fix every non-boundary vertex at depth exactlyLLto be unoccupied.5:Compute occupation ratios bottom-up onTSAW(L)​(G,v)T_{\mathrm{SAW}}^{(L)}(G,v)usingRx=λx​∏y∈children​(x)11+Ry,\displaystyle R_{x}=\lambda_{x}\prod_{y\in\mathrm{children}(x)}\frac{1}{1+R_{y}},withRx=0R_{x}=0for leaves fixed unoccupied andRx=∞R_{x}=\inftyfor leaves fixed occupied.6:LetR~v\widetilde{R}_{v}be the ratio computed at the root andreturnp~v=R~v1+R~v.\widetilde{p}_{v}=\frac{\widetilde{R}_{v}}{1+\widetilde{R}_{v}}.

It is clear that Algorithm2is computationally efficient. Our next step is to show that it is applicable. We will accomplish this by showing that replacing
the full SAW tree by its depth-LLtruncation has a negligible effect on the root ratio. This will follow fromstrong spatial
mixing(SSM), which we define next.

## Definition 2.6.

Fix a vertexv∈V​(G)v\in V(G). We say that the model satisfies strong spatial mixing if there exist constantsC,c>0C,c>0such that whenever two boundary conditionsτ\tauandτ′\tau^{\prime}onTSAW​(G,v)T_{\mathrm{SAW}}(G,v)first differ only at distance at leastℓ\ellfrom the root, their induced root occupation ratios differ by at most|Rvτ−Rvτ′|≤C​e−c​ℓ.|R_{v}^{\tau}-R_{v}^{\tau^{\prime}}|\leq Ce^{-c\ell}.(2.4)

## Remark 2.7.

Once SSM (2.4) is established, Algorithm2can be used as anϵ/n\epsilon/nmarginal oracle for the root occupation probabilitiesμG,𝛌​(σv=1)=Rv1+Rv\mu_{G,\bm{\lambda}}(\sigma_{v}=1)=\frac{R_{v}}{1+R_{v}}. This is seen as follows.

Letτ(L)\tau^{(L)}denote the boundary condition on the full SAW tree
that fixes every non-boundary vertex at distance exactlyLLfrom the
root to be unoccupied. Then the truncated computation produces the root ratioRvτ(L)R_{v}^{\tau^{(L)}}, while the true marginal corresponds to the root ratioRvR_{v}on the full SAW tree. Since these two boundary conditions first differ only at distance at leastLLfrom the root, (2.4) applies and we have|Rvτ(L)−Rv|≤C​e−c​L.|R_{v}^{\tau^{(L)}}-R_{v}|\leq Ce^{-cL}.

Since the mapR↦R/(1+R)R\mapsto R/(1+R)is11-Lipschitz on[0,∞)[0,\infty), the same bound holds for the corresponding marginal:|p~v−μG,𝛌​(σv=1)|≤C​e−c​L.\left|\widetilde{p}_{v}-\mu_{G,\bm{\lambda}}(\sigma_{v}=1)\right|\leq Ce^{-cL}.Therefore, choosingL≥1c​log⁡(C​nϵ)L\geq\frac{1}{c}\log\!\left(\frac{Cn}{\epsilon}\right)ensures additive error at mostϵ/n\epsilon/n. Finally, because the maximum degree is at mostΔ\Delta, the depth-LLtruncated SAW tree has at most1+Δ​∑j=0L−1(Δ−1)j1+\Delta\sum_{j=0}^{L-1}(\Delta-1)^{j}vertices, and therefore has sizeO​(ΔL)O(\Delta^{L}). SinceΔ\Deltais fixed andL=O​(log⁡(n/ϵ))L=O(\log(n/\epsilon)), Algorithm2runs in time polynomial inn/ϵn/\epsilon.

We now specialize the preceding discussion to the tilted hard-core model (1.3) and verify (2.4).

## Theorem 2.8.

FixΔ≥3\Delta\geq 3,λ<λc​(Δ)\lambda<\lambda_{c}(\Delta), andt∈ℝt\in\mathbb{R}. Then there exist constantsC,c>0C,c>0, depending only on(Δ,λ,t)(\Delta,\lambda,t), such that for every bipartite graphG=(L⊔R,E)G=(L\sqcup R,E)of maximum degree at mostΔ\Delta, every vertexv∈V​(G)v\in V(G), ifτ,τ′\tau,\tau^{\prime}are two boundary conditions onTSAW​(G,v)T_{\mathrm{SAW}}(G,v)and𝒟⊆V​(TSAW​(G,v))\mathcal{D}\subseteq V(T_{\mathrm{SAW}}(G,v))is their disagreement set, then|Rvτ−Rvτ′|≤C​e−c​dTSAW​(v,𝒟),|R_{v}^{\tau}-R_{v}^{\tau^{\prime}}|\leq Ce^{-c\,d_{T_{\mathrm{SAW}}}(v,\mathcal{D})},

wheredTSAW​(v,𝒟)d_{T_{\mathrm{SAW}}}(v,\mathcal{D})denotes the minimum distance inTSAW​(G,v)T_{\mathrm{SAW}}(G,v)from the rootvvto a vertex of𝒟\mathcal{D}.

Moreover, for every compact intervalK⊂ℝK\subset\mathbb{R}, the constantsC,cC,cmay be chosen depending only onΔ,λ,K\Delta,\lambda,K, uniformly
for allt∈Kt\in K.

The rest of this section is devoted to proving Theorem2.8. We introduce some notation towards this end. On a rooted tree, suppose a vertexuuis in†∈{L,R}\dagger\in\left\{L,R\right\}, has activityλ†\lambda_{\dagger}, and hasddchildren with occupation ratiosR1,…,RdR_{1},\dots,R_{d}, whered≤Δ−1d\leq\Delta-1. Then the hard-core recursion isRu=λ†​∏i=1d11+Ri.R_{u}=\lambda_{\dagger}\prod_{i=1}^{d}\frac{1}{1+R_{i}}.(2.5)

We will study this recursive map abstractly, so it is useful to introduce the one-level update mapsF†,d​(x1,…,xd):=λ†​∏i=1d11+xi,andf†,d​(x):=λ†(1+x)d,F_{\dagger,d}(x_{1},\dots,x_{d}):=\lambda_{\dagger}\prod_{i=1}^{d}\frac{1}{1+x_{i}},\qquad\text{and}\qquad f_{\dagger,d}(x):=\frac{\lambda_{\dagger}}{(1+x)^{d}},

wheref†,df_{\dagger,d}is the specialization ofF†,dF_{\dagger,d}when all child ratios are equal.

We will use the coordinate changeϕ​(x):=arcsinh⁡x,\phi(x):=\operatorname{arcsinh}\sqrt{x},

and show that the recursive maps are contractive in this new coordinate system.

The one-level update inϕ\phi-coordinates for the equal-input setting isg†,d=ϕ∘f†,d∘ϕ−1g_{\dagger,d}=\phi\circ f_{\dagger,d}\circ\phi^{-1}, i.e.g†,d​(w)=arcsinh⁡(λ†​sechd⁡w).g_{\dagger,d}(w)=\operatorname{arcsinh}\!\left(\sqrt{\lambda_{\dagger}}\,\operatorname{sech}^{d}w\right).

Intuitively, it will be convenient to study contractivity of a self-map that goes from†∈{L,R}\dagger\in\left\{L,R\right\}to†c\dagger^{c}and back to†\dagger. On the tree, this corresponds to a two-level map going in a boundary-to-root direction. Suppose a particular vertexvvis in†\dagger, hasaachildren, and each child hasbbchildren (so grandchildren ofvv). If all grandchild ratios are equal tox1x_{1}, then the intermediate child ratio and the ratio atvvare respectivelyy0:=f†c,b​(x1)=λ†c​(1+x1)−b,x0:=f†,a​(y0)=λ†​(1+y0)−a.y_{0}:=f_{\dagger^{c},b}(x_{1})=\lambda_{\dagger^{c}}(1+x_{1})^{-b},\qquad x_{0}:=f_{\dagger,a}(y_{0})=\lambda_{\dagger}(1+y_{0})^{-a}.

Inϕ\phi-coordinates, define the two-level maph†,a,b:=g†,a∘g†c,b=ϕ∘f†,a∘f†c,b∘ϕ−1.h_{\dagger,a,b}:=g_{\dagger,a}\circ g_{\dagger^{c},b}=\phi\circ f_{\dagger,a}\circ f_{\dagger^{c},b}\circ\phi^{-1}.

The derivative ofh†,a,bh_{\dagger,a,b}atw=ϕ​(x1)w=\phi(x_{1}), in terms of the original coordinates, ish†,a,b′​(ϕ​(x1))=a​b​x11+x1​y01+y0​x01+x0.h^{\prime}_{\dagger,a,b}(\phi(x_{1}))=ab\sqrt{\frac{x_{1}}{1+x_{1}}}\frac{y_{0}}{1+y_{0}}\sqrt{\frac{x_{0}}{1+x_{0}}}.(2.6)

Our analysis centers around this functionh†,a,b′h^{\prime}_{\dagger,a,b}, which measures how much a perturbation at the grandchildren can affect the root after passing through two recursive updates.

The proof of Theorem2.8has three main ingredients. Proposition2.9starts with the general multivariate two-level update obtained from the mapsF†,aF_{\dagger,a}andF†c,biF_{\dagger^{c},b_{i}}, with arbitrary grandchild ratios, and bounds its Jacobian norm by the equal-input functionh†,a,bh_{\dagger,a,b}. Proposition2.10then proves that the derivatives ofh†,a,bh_{\dagger,a,b}are uniformly smaller than11wheneverλ<λc​(Δ)\lambda<\lambda_{c}(\Delta)over the tilt parametertt. Finally, Theorem2.8is proved by iterating this two-level contraction to give exponential decay in theϕ\phi-coordinates, whence Lemma2.11converts that decay back to the original coordinates.

We define analogous notation for the non-equal input recursions. Consider a rooted two-level bipartite tree fragment in which the root belongs to†\dagger, hasaachildren, and each childiihasbib_{i}children, with corresponding grandchild ratiosx1,i,1,…,x1,i,bi≥0x_{1,i,1},\dots,x_{1,i,b_{i}}\geq 0. Define the child ratiosy1,…,yay_{1},\dots,y_{a}and root ratiox0x_{0}byyi=λ†c​∏j=1bi(1+x1,i,j)−1,x0=λ†​∏i=1a(1+yi)−1.\displaystyle y_{i}=\lambda_{\dagger^{c}}\prod_{j=1}^{b_{i}}(1+x_{1,i,j})^{-1},\qquad x_{0}=\lambda_{\dagger}\prod_{i=1}^{a}(1+y_{i})^{-1}.(2.7)

In the correspondingϕ\phi-coordinates write the grandchild, child, and root ratios asq1,i,j=ϕ​(x1,i,j),ri=ϕ​(yi),andq0=ϕ​(x0).\displaystyle q_{1,i,j}=\phi(x_{1,i,j}),\qquad r_{i}=\phi(y_{i}),\qquad\text{and}\qquad q_{0}=\phi(x_{0}).(2.8)

Given an arbitrary input of unequal number of grandchildren and unequal grandchild ratios{x1,i,j}1≤i≤a,1≤j≤bi\left\{x_{1,i,j}\right\}_{1\leq i\leq a,1\leq j\leq b_{i}}, we will now outline a construction of a corresponding equal-input effective grandchild ratiox1x_{1}. We also setb=max1≤i≤a⁡bib=\max_{1\leq i\leq a}b_{i}, and setb=0b=0ifa=0a=0. In Proposition2.9the influence of the grandchild ratios on the root ratio for arbitrary unequal inputs will be shown to be bounded by that of the equal input setting with grandchild ratiox1x_{1}, i.e. by the magnitude ofh†,a,b′​(ϕ​(x1))h^{\prime}_{\dagger,a,b}(\phi(x_{1})). In the case wherea,b>0a,b>0and both activities are positive, setx1=(λ†cy0)1/b−1,wherey0=(∏i=1a(1+yi))1/a−1.\displaystyle x_{1}=\left(\frac{\lambda_{\dagger^{c}}}{y_{0}}\right)^{1/b}-1,\qquad\text{where}\qquad y_{0}=\left(\prod_{i=1}^{a}(1+y_{i})\right)^{1/a}-1.(2.9)

Thisy0y_{0}is chosen to preserve the root ratio so thatx0=λ†​(1+y0)−ax_{0}=\lambda_{\dagger}(1+y_{0})^{-a}, and the equal-input grandchild ratiox1x_{1}is chosen to preserve this child ratioy0y_{0}so thaty0=λ†c​(1+x1)−by_{0}=\lambda_{\dagger^{c}}(1+x_{1})^{-b}. In the degenerate casesa=0a=0,b=0b=0, or one of the two activities is0, we takex1=0x_{1}=0.

## Proposition 2.9.

On a rooted two-level bipartite tree fragment with root in†\daggerhavingaachildren, and each childiihavingbib_{i}children, suppose the grandchild ratios are{x1,i,j}1≤i≤a,1≤j≤bi\left\{x_{1,i,j}\right\}_{1\leq i\leq a,1\leq j\leq b_{i}}. Then withx1x_{1}defined in (2.9), we have∑i=1a∑j=1bi|∂q0∂q1,i,j|≤h†,a,b′​(ϕ​(x1)).\sum_{i=1}^{a}\sum_{j=1}^{b_{i}}\left|\frac{\partial q_{0}}{\partial q_{1,i,j}}\right|\leq h^{\prime}_{\dagger,a,b}(\phi(x_{1})).

For anyλ<λc​(Δ)\lambda<\lambda_{c}(\Delta), we havemax†⁣∈{L,R}⁡max0≤a,b≤Δ−1​supt∈ℝsupx1≥0h†,a,b′​(ϕ​(x1))<1.\max_{\dagger\in\{L,R\}}\max_{0\leq a,b\leq\Delta-1}\ \sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\sup_{x_{1}\geq 0}h^{\prime}_{\dagger,a,b}(\phi(x_{1}))<1.

## Lemma 2.11.

Suppose there exist constantsC0,c>0C_{0},c>0such that for any two boundary conditionsτ,τ′\tau,\tau^{\prime},|ϕ​(Rvτ)−ϕ​(Rvτ′)|≤C0​e−c​d​(v,𝒟),\bigl|\phi(R_{v}^{\tau})-\phi(R_{v}^{\tau^{\prime}})\bigr|\leq C_{0}e^{-c\,d(v,\mathcal{D})},

where𝒟\mathcal{D}is the set on which the boundary conditions differ. Suppose also that there is a uniform bound0≤Rvτ,Rvτ′≤Rmax0\leq R_{v}^{\tau},\;R_{v}^{\tau^{\prime}}\leq R_{\max}.
Then there exists a constantC′=C′​(Rmax,C0)>0C^{\prime}=C^{\prime}(R_{\max},C_{0})>0such that|Rvτ−Rvτ′|≤C′​e−c​d​(v,𝒟).|R_{v}^{\tau}-R_{v}^{\tau^{\prime}}|\leq C^{\prime}e^{-c\,d(v,\mathcal{D})}.

## Proof.

SinceRvτ,Rvτ′∈[0,Rmax]R_{v}^{\tau},R_{v}^{\tau^{\prime}}\in[0,R_{\max}]and the inverse mapϕ−1​(s)=sinh2⁡s\phi^{-1}(s)=\sinh^{2}sis smooth on the compact interval[0,ϕ​(Rmax)][0,\phi(R_{\max})], we haveLRmax:=sup0≤s≤ϕ​(Rmax)|(ϕ−1)′​(s)|<∞.L_{R_{\max}}:=\sup_{0\leq s\leq\phi(R_{\max})}\left|\left(\phi^{-1}\right)^{\prime}(s)\right|<\infty.The mean value theorem gives|Rvτ−Rvτ′|≤LRmax​|ϕ​(Rvτ)−ϕ​(Rvτ′)|≤LRmax​C0​e−c​d​(v,𝒟).∎\bigl|R_{v}^{\tau}-R_{v}^{\tau^{\prime}}\bigr|\leq L_{R_{\max}}\,\bigl|\phi(R_{v}^{\tau})-\phi(R_{v}^{\tau^{\prime}})\bigr|\leq L_{R_{\max}}C_{0}e^{-c\,d(v,\mathcal{D})}.\qed

with the right-hand side interpreted as0ifuuhas no grandchildren.

SinceM<1M<1by Proposition2.10, the right-hand

It remains to return to the actual SAW tree. The rootvvofTSAWT_{\mathrm{SAW}}may have as many asΔ\Deltachildren, but each child subtree has forward degree at mostd=Δ−1d=\Delta-1. the prefactor. Ifℓ≥2\ell\geq 2, we first apply a one-level mean value
bound at the root (this nuisance arises because the SAW tree root may
haveΔ\Deltachildren, instead ofΔ−1\Delta-1children.) Sinceℓ≥2\ell\geq 2, any child fixed by either boundary condition is fixed in
the same way by both. A child fixed to be occupied under both conditions
forces both root ratios to be0, while a child fixed to be
unoccupied under both may be omitted from the recursion. Thus we may

that theℓ∞\ell_{\infty}Lipschitz constant of the root update inϕ\phi-coordinates is at mostL1:=Δ​Λ1+Λ.L_{1}:=\Delta\,\frac{\Lambda}{1+\Lambda}.The boundary conditions induced on each remaining child subtree first differ at distance at leastℓ−1\ell-1from that child. Therefore, using (2.12),|ϕ​(Rvτ)−ϕ​(Rvτ′)|≤L1​Aℓ−1≤C0​e−c​ℓ\bigl|\phi(R_{v}^{\tau})-\phi(R_{v}^{\tau^{\prime}})\bigr|\leq L_{1}A_{\ell-1}\leq C_{0}e^{-c\,\ell}

for constantsC0<∞C_{0}<\inftyandc>0c>0depending only on(Δ,λ,t)(\Delta,\lambda,t). Hence all the constants above may be chosen uniformly fort∈Kt\in K.

## Proof of Proposition2.9.

## Proof of Proposition2.9.

Ifa=0a=0,b=0b=0, or one of the two activities is0, there is nothing to prove. Pad each childiiwith dummy grandchildrenx1,i,j=0x_{1,i,j}=0forj>bij>b_{i}. This does not changeyiy_{i},x0x_{0}, or the Jacobian sum. Thus we may assume without loss every child has exactlybbgrandchildren.

A direct chain-rule computation gives∑i=1a∑j=1b|∂q0∂q1,i,j|=x01+x0​∑i=1ayi1+yi​∑j=1bx1,i,j1+x1,i,j.\sum_{i=1}^{a}\sum_{j=1}^{b}\left|\frac{\partial q_{0}}{\partial q_{1,i,j}}\right|=\sqrt{\frac{x_{0}}{1+x_{0}}}\sum_{i=1}^{a}\frac{y_{i}}{1+y_{i}}\sum_{j=1}^{b}\sqrt{\frac{x_{1,i,j}}{1+x_{1,i,j}}}.(2.13)

The argument to pass to equal inputs is essentially two applications of Jensen’s inequality.

First fixii. We will use a change of variablesuj:=log⁡(1+x1,i,j)u_{j}:=\log(1+x_{1,i,j}). DefineF​(u)=1−e−uF(u)=\sqrt{1-e^{-u}}and note thatFFis concave onu≥0u\geq 0. Thus by Jensen’s inequality, we have∑j=1bx1,i,j1+x1,i,j=∑j=1bF​(uj)≤b⋅F​(1b​∑j=1buj)=b​x1,i1+x1,i,\sum_{j=1}^{b}\sqrt{\frac{x_{1,i,j}}{1+x_{1,i,j}}}=\sum_{j=1}^{b}F(u_{j})\leq b\cdot F\!\left(\frac{1}{b}\sum_{j=1}^{b}u_{j}\right)=b\sqrt{\frac{x_{1,i}}{1+x_{1,i}}},

where we definex1,i:=(∏j=1b(1+x1,i,j))1/b−1.\displaystyle x_{1,i}:=\left(\prod_{j=1}^{b}(1+x_{1,i,j})\right)^{1/b}-1.

We may understandx1,ix_{1,i}as the equalized grandchild ratios of theii-th child, chosen so that the ratioyi=λ†c​(1+x1,i)−by_{i}=\lambda_{\dagger^{c}}(1+x_{1,i})^{-b}is preserved. Thus (2.13) becomes∑i=1a∑j=1b|∂q0∂q1,i,j|≤b​x01+x0​∑i=1ayi1+yi​x1,i1+x1,i.\sum_{i=1}^{a}\sum_{j=1}^{b}\left|\frac{\partial q_{0}}{\partial q_{1,i,j}}\right|\leq b\sqrt{\frac{x_{0}}{1+x_{0}}}\sum_{i=1}^{a}\frac{y_{i}}{1+y_{i}}\sqrt{\frac{x_{1,i}}{1+x_{1,i}}}.(2.14)

Next setvi:=log⁡(1+yi)v_{i}:=\log(1+y_{i}). Theii-th summand in (2.14) isK​(vi):=(1−e−vi)​1−(evi−1λ†c)1/b.K(v_{i}):=(1-e^{-v_{i}})\sqrt{1-\left(\frac{e^{v_{i}}-1}{\lambda_{\dagger^{c}}}\right)^{1/b}}.

We claim thatKKis concave. This is seen as follows. Defineα=(ev−1λ†c)1/b∈(0,1),\alpha=\left(\frac{e^{v}-1}{\lambda_{\dagger^{c}}}\right)^{1/b}\in(0,1),then one hasK′′​(v)=−N​(α)4​λ†c​b2​αb​(1−α)3/2​(1+λ†c​αb),K^{\prime\prime}(v)=-\frac{N(\alpha)}{4\lambda_{\dagger^{c}}b^{2}\alpha^{b}(1-\alpha)^{3/2}(1+\lambda_{\dagger^{c}}\alpha^{b})},

whereN​(α)\displaystyle N(\alpha)=λ†c2​α2​b+1​(2−α)+4​λ†c​b2​αb​(1−α)2+2​λ†c​b​αb+1​(1−α)\displaystyle=\lambda_{\dagger^{c}}^{2}\alpha^{2b+1}(2-\alpha)+4\lambda_{\dagger^{c}}b^{2}\alpha^{b}(1-\alpha)^{2}+2\lambda_{\dagger^{c}}b\,\alpha^{b+1}(1-\alpha)+2​λ†c​αb+1​(2−α)+α​(2​b​(1−α)+(2−α))>0.\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+2\lambda_{\dagger^{c}}\alpha^{b+1}(2-\alpha)+\alpha\bigl(2b(1-\alpha)+(2-\alpha)\bigr)>0.

Thus by Jensen’s inequality again,∑i=1ayi1+yi​x1,i1+x1,i≤a​y01+y0​x11+x1,\sum_{i=1}^{a}\frac{y_{i}}{1+y_{i}}\sqrt{\frac{x_{1,i}}{1+x_{1,i}}}\leq a\,\frac{y_{0}}{1+y_{0}}\sqrt{\frac{x_{1}}{1+x_{1}}},

wherey0y_{0}andx1x_{1}are defined as in (2.9).
Note thatx0=λ†​(1+y0)−ax_{0}=\lambda_{\dagger}(1+y_{0})^{-a}. Substituting into (2.14), we see from (2.6) that this yields the desired∑i=1a∑j=1b|∂q0∂q1,i,j|≤a​b​x11+x1​y01+y0​x01+x0=h†,a,b′​(ϕ​(x1)).∎\sum_{i=1}^{a}\sum_{j=1}^{b}\left|\frac{\partial q_{0}}{\partial q_{1,i,j}}\right|\leq ab\sqrt{\frac{x_{1}}{1+x_{1}}}\frac{y_{0}}{1+y_{0}}\sqrt{\frac{x_{0}}{1+x_{0}}}=h^{\prime}_{\dagger,a,b}(\phi(x_{1})).\qed

## Proof of Proposition2.10.

In light of Proposition2.9, to show contraction of the two-level map for arbitrary grandchild ratios, we can focus our attention on the equal-input scalar derivativeh†,a,b′h^{\prime}_{\dagger,a,b}in (2.6). It is equivalent and slightly cleaner to bound the square of this derivative,(h†,a,b′​(ϕ​(x1)))2\bigl(h^{\prime}_{\dagger,a,b}(\phi(x_{1}))\bigr)^{2}, which can be written asΘa,b,λ​(y0,x1):=a2​b2​λ2​y02​x1(1+y0)2​(1+x1)​(λ2+y0​(1+y0)a​(1+x1)b).\Theta_{a,b,\lambda}(y_{0},x_{1}):=a^{2}b^{2}\,\frac{\lambda^{2}y_{0}^{2}x_{1}}{(1+y_{0})^{2}(1+x_{1})\bigl(\lambda^{2}+y_{0}(1+y_{0})^{a}(1+x_{1})^{b}\bigr)}.(2.15)

This section establishes a zero-freeness result for a complex version ofZG​(λ;t)Z_{G}(\lambda;t). This will be used to establish a local CLT for the balanceB​(I)B(I)under the tilted measure.

## 2.5A local central limit theorem for the imbalance

Throughout this section, letG=(L⊔R,E)G=(L\sqcup R,E)be a bipartite graph of maximum degree at mostΔ\Delta, and writen:=|V​(G)|=|L|+|R|n:=|V(G)|=|L|+|R|.

We now prove a local central limit theorem for the imbalanceB​(I)B(I)(defined in (1.2)) under the
tilted hard-core measure. This will be crucial for the rejection sampling
step in Algorithm1. Throughout, letN​(x):=e−x2/22​πN(x):=\frac{e^{-x^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}

denote the density of the standard normal distribution. The main result for this section is as follows.

## Theorem 2.17.

FixΔ≥3\Delta\geq 3,δ>0\delta>0,t0>0t_{0}>0, andλ∈(0,λc​(Δ)−δ)\lambda\in(0,\lambda_{c}(\Delta)-\delta).
LetI∼μλ,tI\sim\mu_{\lambda,t}for somet∈[−t0,t0]t\in[-t_{0},t_{0}]. Letμ:=𝔼μλ,t​[B​(I)],σ2:=𝖵𝖺𝗋μλ,t⁡[B​(I)].\mu:=\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\lambda,t}}[B(I)],\qquad\sigma^{2}:=\operatorname{\mathsf{Var}}_{\mu_{\lambda,t}}[B(I)].

Thensupb∈ℤ|σ−1​N​(b−μσ)−ℙμλ,t​[B​(I)=b]|=OΔ,δ,t0,λ​((log⁡n)5/2n).\sup_{b\in\mathbb{Z}}\left|\sigma^{-1}N\!\left(\frac{b-\mu}{\sigma}\right)-\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{\lambda,t}}[B(I)=b]\right|=O_{\Delta,\delta,t_{0},\lambda}\!\left(\frac{(\log n)^{5/2}}{n}\right).

The proof proceeds by the following standard Fourier inversion estimate.

## Lemma 2.18.

(see Lemma 2.5 of[24]or Lemma 3 of[2]) 
LetXXbe a random variable supported on the latticeL=α+σ−1​ℤL=\alpha+\sigma^{-1}\mathbb{Z}for someα∈ℝ\alpha\in\mathbb{R}andσ>0\sigma>0, and letZ∼𝒩​(0,1)Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1). Thensupx∈L|σ−1​N​(x)−ℙ​[X=x]|≤1σ​∫−π​σπ​σ|𝔼​[ei​θ​X]−𝔼​[ei​θ​Z]|​𝑑θ+e−π2​σ2/2.\sup_{x\in L}\left|\sigma^{-1}N(x)-\mathbb{P}[X=x]\right|\leq\frac{1}{\sigma}\int_{-\pi\sigma}^{\pi\sigma}\left|\mathbb{E}\!\left[e^{i\theta X}\right]-\mathbb{E}\!\left[e^{i\theta Z}\right]\right|\,d\theta+e^{-\pi^{2}\sigma^{2}/2}.

The proof follows the strategy of Section 3 of[24]. WritingX=(B​(I)−μ)/σX=(B(I)-\mu)/\sigma,ϕX​(θ):=𝔼​[ei​θ​X]\phi_{X}(\theta):=\mathbb{E}[e^{i\theta X}], andϕZ​(θ):=e−θ2/2\phi_{Z}(\theta):=e^{-\theta^{2}/2}, the Fourier integral in
Lemma2.18is split at a cutoffθ0≍log⁡n\theta_{0}\asymp\sqrt{\log n}as∫−π​σπ​σ|ϕX​(θ)−ϕZ​(θ)|​𝑑θ=∫|θ|≤θ0|ϕX​(θ)−ϕZ​(θ)|​𝑑θ⏟low Fourier phases+∫θ0<|θ|≤π​σ|ϕX​(θ)−ϕZ​(θ)|​𝑑θ⏟high Fourier phases.\int_{-\pi\sigma}^{\pi\sigma}\bigl|\phi_{X}(\theta)-\phi_{Z}(\theta)\bigr|\,d\theta=\underbrace{\int_{|\theta|\leq\theta_{0}}\bigl|\phi_{X}(\theta)-\phi_{Z}(\theta)\bigr|\,d\theta}_{\text{low Fourier phases}}+\underbrace{\int_{\theta_{0}<|\theta|\leq\pi\sigma}\bigl|\phi_{X}(\theta)-\phi_{Z}(\theta)\bigr|\,d\theta}_{\text{high Fourier phases}}.

The low frequency part is controlled by the zero-freeness result from the previous subsection, similar to the proof of Lemma 3.3 in[24]. The high frequency part is bounded by conditioning on a collection of
well-separated vertices, adapting the arguments in[24]Lemma 3.5. Note that[24]gives a local CLT for|I|\left|I\right|in a univariate hard-core model, which differs from our variable of interestB​(I)B(I)and our tilted model with different fugacities inLLandRR.

Following[24], we begin with a
variance bound.

## Lemma 2.19.

Letλ∈(0,λc​(Δ)−δ)\lambda\in(0,\lambda_{c}(\Delta)-\delta)andt∈[−t0,t0]t\in[-t_{0},t_{0}].
Then there exist constantscΔ,δ,t0,CΔ,δ,t0>0c_{\Delta,\delta,t_{0}},C_{\Delta,\delta,t_{0}}>0such thatcΔ,δ,t0​λ​n≤𝖵𝖺𝗋μλ,t⁡[B​(I)]≤CΔ,δ,t0​n.c_{\Delta,\delta,t_{0}}\,\lambda n\;\leq\;\operatorname{\mathsf{Var}}_{\mu_{\lambda,t}}[B(I)]\;\leq\;C_{\Delta,\delta,t_{0}}\,n.

## Proof.

We first prove the upper bound. SinceB​(I)B(I)may be negative, we pass fromZG​(λ;t,ζ)Z_{G}(\lambda;t,\zeta)to the polynomialFt​(ζ):=ζ|R|​ZG​(λ;t,ζ)=∑I∈ℐGλ|I|​et​B​(I)​ζB​(I)+|R|.F_{t}(\zeta):=\zeta^{|R|}Z_{G}(\lambda;t,\zeta)=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{G}}\lambda^{|I|}e^{\,tB(I)}\zeta^{B(I)+|R|}.

Because−|R|≤B​(I)≤|L|,-|R|\leq B(I)\leq|L|,the exponentB​(I)+|R|B(I)+|R|lies in{0,1,…,n}\{0,1,\dots,n\}, soFtF_{t}is a polynomial of degree at mostnn.
By Proposition2.15, there existsρ=ρ​(Δ,δ,t0)∈(0,1)\rho=\rho(\Delta,\delta,t_{0})\in(0,1)such thatZG​(λ;t,ζ)≠0Z_{G}(\lambda;t,\zeta)\neq 0for all|ζ−1|<ρ|\zeta-1|<\rho.
Thus,FtF_{t}also has no zeros in{|ζ−1|<ρ}\{|\zeta-1|<\rho\}. Moreover,Ft​(0)=λ|R|​e−t​|R|≠0F_{t}(0)=\lambda^{|R|}e^{-t|R|}\neq 0. Thus, we writeFt​(ζ)=at​∏j=1N(1−ζζj),F_{t}(\zeta)=a_{t}\prod_{j=1}^{N}\left(1-\frac{\zeta}{\zeta_{j}}\right),

whereN≤nN\leq nandζ1,…,ζN\zeta_{1},\dots,\zeta_{N}are the nonzero roots ofFtF_{t}, counted with multiplicity.
Then everyζj\zeta_{j}satisfies|ζj−1|≥ρ.|\zeta_{j}-1|\geq\rho.Now, observe that (with some overloaded notation)ZG​(λ;t+s)=∑I∈ℐGλ|I|​e(t+s)​B​(I)=ZG​(λ;t,es),Z_{G}(\lambda;t+s)=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{G}}\lambda^{|I|}e^{(t+s)B(I)}=Z_{G}(\lambda;t,e^{s}),

and sinceFt​(es)=es​|R|​ZG​(λ;t,es)F_{t}(e^{s})=e^{s|R|}Z_{G}(\lambda;t,e^{s}),
we havelog⁡Ft​(es)=s​|R|+log⁡ZG​(λ;t,es)\log F_{t}(e^{s})=s|R|+\log Z_{G}(\lambda;t,e^{s}).
Therefore𝖵𝖺𝗋μλ,t⁡[B​(I)]=d2d​s2​log⁡ZG​(λ;t+s)|s=0=d2d​s2​log⁡Ft​(es)|s=0.\operatorname{\mathsf{Var}}_{\mu_{\lambda,t}}[B(I)]=\left.\frac{d^{2}}{ds^{2}}\log Z_{G}(\lambda;t+s)\right|_{s=0}=\left.\frac{d^{2}}{ds^{2}}\log F_{t}(e^{s})\right|_{s=0}.

Using the factorization ofFtF_{t}, we have thatlog⁡Ft​(es)=log⁡at+∑j=1Nlog⁡(1−esζj),\log F_{t}(e^{s})=\log a_{t}+\sum_{j=1}^{N}\log\!\left(1-\frac{e^{s}}{\zeta_{j}}\right),sodd​s​log⁡Ft​(es)=−∑j=1Nesζj−esandd2d​s2​log⁡Ft​(es)=−∑j=1Nes​ζj(ζj−es)2.\displaystyle\frac{d}{ds}\log F_{t}(e^{s})=-\sum_{j=1}^{N}\frac{e^{s}}{\zeta_{j}-e^{s}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\frac{d^{2}}{ds^{2}}\log F_{t}(e^{s})=-\sum_{j=1}^{N}\frac{e^{s}\zeta_{j}}{(\zeta_{j}-e^{s})^{2}}.(2.28)

Evaluating ats=0s=0, we obtain𝖵𝖺𝗋μλ,t⁡[B​(I)]=−∑j=1Nζj(ζj−1)2≤∑j=1N|ζj(ζj−1)2|.\operatorname{\mathsf{Var}}_{\mu_{\lambda,t}}[B(I)]=-\sum_{j=1}^{N}\frac{\zeta_{j}}{(\zeta_{j}-1)^{2}}\leq\sum_{j=1}^{N}\left|\frac{\zeta_{j}}{(\zeta_{j}-1)^{2}}\right|.

Since|ζj|≤|ζj−1|+1|\zeta_{j}|\leq|\zeta_{j}-1|+1and|ζj−1|≥ρ|\zeta_{j}-1|\geq\rho, each summand is at mostρ−1+ρ−2=Oρ​(1)\rho^{-1}+\rho^{-2}=O_{\rho}(1). AsN≤nN\leq n, this gives𝖵𝖺𝗋μλ,t⁡[B​(I)]≤Cρ​n\operatorname{\mathsf{Var}}_{\mu_{\lambda,t}}[B(I)]\leq C_{\rho}n, proving the upper bound.

We now prove the lower bound. ChooseJ∈{L,R}J\in\{L,R\}so that|J|≥n/2|J|\geq n/2,
writeJ¯:=V​(G)∖J\bar{J}:=V(G)\setminus J, and setK:=I∩J¯K:=I\cap\bar{J}. LetλJ\lambda_{J}be the activity onJJ, and setpJ:=λJ/(1+λJ)p_{J}:=\lambda_{J}/(1+\lambda_{J}). SinceGGis bipartite, conditional onKK, the vertices ofJ∖N​(K)J\setminus N(K)are mutually nonadjacent and
unblocked, while the vertices ofN​(K)∩JN(K)\cap Jare forced to be absent. Thus,
withU:=|J∖N​(K)|U:=|J\setminus N(K)|, we have|I∩J|∣K∼Bin⁡(U,pJ)|I\cap J|\mid K\sim\operatorname{Bin}(U,p_{J}). MoreoverB​(I)B(I)differs
from|I∩J||I\cap J|only by a sign and an additive function ofKK. Hence the
law of total variance gives𝖵𝖺𝗋⁡[B​(I)]≥𝔼​[𝖵𝖺𝗋⁡(B​(I)∣K)]=pJ​(1−pJ)​𝔼​U.\operatorname{\mathsf{Var}}[B(I)]\geq\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{\mathsf{Var}}(B(I)\mid K)]=p_{J}(1-p_{J})\,\mathbb{E}U.

It remains to lower bound𝔼​U\mathbb{E}U. Fixu∈Ju\in J, and letv1,…,vdv_{1},\dots,v_{d}be its neighbors inJ¯\bar{J}, whered≤Δd\leq\Delta. Thenℙ(u∈J∖N(K))=∏i=1dℙ(vi∉I|v1,…,vi−1∉I).\mathbb{P}(u\in J\setminus N(K))=\prod_{i=1}^{d}\mathbb{P}\!\left(v_{i}\notin I\,\middle|\,v_{1},\dots,v_{i-1}\notin I\right).

LetλJ¯\lambda_{\bar{J}}be the activity onJ¯\bar{J}, and letAiA_{i}be the
event thatv1,…,vi−1∉Iv_{1},\dots,v_{i-1}\notin I. Deletingviv_{i}from any independent

does not containviv_{i}, with the original weight larger by a factorλJ¯\lambda_{\bar{J}}. Thereforeℙ​(vi∈I∣Ai)≤λJ¯​ℙ​(vi∉I∣Ai),soℙ​(vi∉I∣Ai)≥11+λJ¯.\mathbb{P}(v_{i}\in I\mid A_{i})\leq\lambda_{\bar{J}}\,\mathbb{P}(v_{i}\notin I\mid A_{i}),\qquad\text{so}\qquad\mathbb{P}(v_{i}\notin I\mid A_{i})\geq\frac{1}{1+\lambda_{\bar{J}}}.

Since|t|≤t0|t|\leq t_{0}, bothλJ\lambda_{J}andλJ¯\lambda_{\bar{J}}lie in[λ​e−t0,λ​et0][\lambda e^{-t_{0}},\lambda e^{t_{0}}]. Henceℙ​(u∈J∖N​(K))≥(1+λJ¯)−d≥(1+λ​et0)−Δ,𝔼​U≥n2​(1+λ​et0)−Δ.\mathbb{P}(u\in J\setminus N(K))\geq(1+\lambda_{\bar{J}})^{-d}\geq(1+\lambda e^{t_{0}})^{-\Delta},\qquad\mathbb{E}U\geq\frac{n}{2}(1+\lambda e^{t_{0}})^{-\Delta}.

AlsopJ​(1−pJ)=λJ(1+λJ)2≥λ​e−t0(1+λ​et0)2.p_{J}(1-p_{J})=\frac{\lambda_{J}}{(1+\lambda_{J})^{2}}\geq\frac{\lambda e^{-t_{0}}}{(1+\lambda e^{t_{0}})^{2}}.

Combining these estimates and usingλ≤λc​(Δ)−δ\lambda\leq\lambda_{c}(\Delta)-\delta, we finally obtain𝖵𝖺𝗋μλ,t⁡[B​(I)]≥e−t02​(1+(λc​(Δ)−δ)​et0)2+Δ​λ​n.∎\operatorname{\mathsf{Var}}_{\mu_{\lambda,t}}[B(I)]\geq\frac{e^{-t_{0}}}{2(1+(\lambda_{c}(\Delta)-\delta)e^{t_{0}})^{2+\Delta}}\,\lambda n.\qed

## Low Fourier phases.

Here we use the zero-freeness of the tilted hard-core
model from Proposition2.15as well as the following general result of[32].

## Theorem 2.20(Theorem 1.2 of[32]).

LetXXbe a random variable taking values in{0,1,…,n}\{0,1,\dots,n\}with meanμ\muand varianceσ2\sigma^{2}, and letfX​(ζ)=∑k=0nℙ​[X=k]​ζkf_{X}(\zeta)=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\mathbb{P}[X=k]\zeta^{k}denote its probability generating function. Letξ∗:=min⁡{|ξ−1|:fX​(ξ)=0}\xi_{*}:=\min\{|\xi-1|:f_{X}(\xi)=0\}. Thensupt∈ℝ|ℙ​[X−μσ≤t]−ℙ​[Z≤t]|=O​(log⁡nξ∗​σ).\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\left|\mathbb{P}\!\left[\frac{X-\mu}{\sigma}\leq t\right]-\mathbb{P}[Z\leq t]\right|=O\!\left(\frac{\log n}{\xi_{*}\sigma}\right).

In what follows, we will apply this withμ:=𝔼μλ,t​[B​(I)],σ2:=Varμλ,t⁡(B​(I)),andX:=B​(I)−μσ.\mu:=\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\lambda,t}}[B(I)],\qquad\sigma^{2}:=\operatorname{Var}_{\mu_{\lambda,t}}(B(I)),\qquad\text{and}\qquad X:=\frac{B(I)-\mu}{\sigma}.(2.29)

## Lemma 2.21.

Letλ∈(0,λc​(Δ)−δ)\lambda\in(0,\lambda_{c}(\Delta)-\delta)andt∈[−t0,t0]t\in[-t_{0},t_{0}], and letρ=ρ​(Δ,δ,t0)∈(0,1)\rho=\rho(\Delta,\delta,t_{0})\in(0,1)be as in Proposition2.15.
Then for everyu∈ℝu\in\mathbb{R}, forZ∼𝒩​(0,1)Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1),|𝔼μλ,t​[ei​u​X]−𝔼​[ei​u​Z]|=Oρ​(|u|​(log⁡n)3/2+log⁡nσ).\left|\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\lambda,t}}\!\left[e^{iuX}\right]-\mathbb{E}\!\left[e^{iuZ}\right]\right|=O_{\rho}\!\left(\frac{|u|(\log n)^{3/2}+\log n}{\sigma}\right).

## Proof.

SetY:=B​(I)+|R|Y:=B(I)+|R|. The shift by|R||R|converts theB​(I)B(I)into
an integer-valued variable in{0,…,n}\{0,\ldots,n\}, without changing its variance
or its standardized version. Its probability generating function isfY​(ζ):=𝔼μλ,t​[ζY]=ζ|R|​ZG​(λ;t,ζ)ZG​(λ;t,1).f_{Y}(\zeta):=\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\lambda,t}}[\zeta^{Y}]=\zeta^{|R|}\frac{Z_{G}(\lambda;t,\zeta)}{Z_{G}(\lambda;t,1)}.

By Proposition2.15, theZGZ_{G}factors have no
zero in|ζ−1|<ρ|\zeta-1|<\rho. Theζ|R|\zeta^{\left|R\right|}factor has zeros only atζ=0\zeta=0, which is outside this disk becauseρ<1\rho<1. Hence the nearest zero
parameterξ∗\xi_{*}from Theorem2.20satisfiesξ∗≥ρ\xi_{*}\geq\rho. Moreover,𝔼​[Y]=μ+|R|\mathbb{E}[Y]=\mu+|R|,Var⁡(Y)=σ2\operatorname{Var}(Y)=\sigma^{2}, andY−𝔼​[Y]σ=B​(I)−μσ=X.\frac{Y-\mathbb{E}[Y]}{\sigma}=\frac{B(I)-\mu}{\sigma}=X.Applying Theorem2.20toYYgivessupx∈ℝ|ℙ​[X≤x]−ℙ​[Z≤x]|=Oρ​(log⁡nσ).\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\left|\mathbb{P}[X\leq x]-\mathbb{P}[Z\leq x]\right|=O_{\rho}\!\left(\frac{\log n}{\sigma}\right).

To convert this tail difference estimate into a difference in characteristic functions, we follow the smoothing argument in[24]proof of Lemma 3.3, specifically
equation (3.1) and the displayed calculations immediately following it. The correspondence is:
the variableY=|I|Y=|I|, meanμ\mu, and standard
deviationσ\sigmain[24]are replaced here byY=B​(I)+|R|Y=B(I)+|R|,μ+|R|\mu+|R|, andσ\sigmarespectively. The required zero-freeness result is provided by
Proposition2.15. Using the truncation cutoffτ=8​log⁡n\tau=\sqrt{8\log n}, the calculations are identical and lead to the desired conclusion, so we omit the details.
∎

## High Fourier phases.

The following conditioning argument follows the proof of Lemma 3.5 of[24]; see also[12]. We first require the following general lemma.

## Lemma 2.22(Lemma 3.4 of[24]).

LetG=(V,E)G=(V,E)be a graph onnnvertices with maximum degree at mostΔ\Delta.
Then there exists a subsetS⊆VS\subseteq Vof size|S|=Ω​(nΔ3)|S|=\Omega\!\left(\frac{n}{\Delta^{3}}\right)such that all vertices inSSare pairwise at distance at least44in the graph metric.
Moreover, there is an algorithm to find such a subsetSSin timeOΔ​(n)O_{\Delta}(n).

Letλ∈(0,λc​(Δ)−δ)\lambda\in(0,\lambda_{c}(\Delta)-\delta),t∈[−t0,t0]t\in[-t_{0},t_{0}], andμ\mu,σ\sigma, andXXbe defined as in (2.29).
Then for allθ∈[−π​σ,π​σ]\theta\in[-\pi\sigma,\pi\sigma],|𝔼μλ,t​[e−i​θ​X]|≤exp⁡(−c​(Δ,t0)​λ​n​θ2σ2).\left|\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\lambda,t}}\!\left[e^{-i\theta X}\right]\right|\leq\exp\!\left(-c(\Delta,t_{0})\,\lambda n\,\frac{\theta^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\right).

for the two activities in the present setting. It suffices to prove|𝔼μλ,t​[e−i​u​B​(I)]|≤exp⁡(−c​λ​n​u2)for all​u∈[−π,π],\left|\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\lambda,t}}\!\left[e^{-iuB(I)}\right]\right|\leq\exp(-c\lambda nu^{2})\qquad\text{for all }u\in[-\pi,\pi],(2.30)

for somec=c​(Δ,t0)>0c=c(\Delta,t_{0})>0. Indeed, substitutingu=θ/σu=\theta/\sigmaand using𝔼μλ,t​[e−i​θ​X]=ei​θ​μ/σ​𝔼μλ,t​[e−i​(θ/σ)​B​(I)]\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\lambda,t}}\!\left[e^{-i\theta X}\right]=e^{i\theta\mu/\sigma}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\lambda,t}}\!\left[e^{-i(\theta/\sigma)B(I)}\right]

gives the stated bound whenever|θ|≤π​σ|\theta|\leq\pi\sigma.

Fixu∈[−π,π]u\in[-\pi,\pi]. The activities satisfy0<λ​e−t0≤λL​(t),λR​(t)≤λc​(Δ)​et0.0<\lambda e^{-t_{0}}\leq\lambda_{L}(t),\lambda_{R}(t)\leq\lambda_{c}(\Delta)e^{t_{0}}.

ChooseSSas in Lemma2.22, so that|S|=ΩΔ​(n)|S|=\Omega_{\Delta}(n)and distinct vertices ofSShave distance at
least44. LetT:={x:distG⁡(x,S)≥2}T:=\{x:\operatorname{dist}_{G}(x,S)\geq 2\}, sampleJ:=I∩TJ:=I\cap T, and condition onJJ. Forv∈Sv\in S, writeHv:=G​[{v}∪N​(v)],Av​(J):={w∈N​(v):N​(w)∩J=∅},mv:=|Av​(J)|.H_{v}:=G[\{v\}\cup N(v)],\qquad A_{v}(J):=\{w\in N(v):N(w)\cap J=\varnothing\},\qquad m_{v}:=|A_{v}(J)|.

ThusHvH_{v}is the graph consisting ofvvand its neighbours, andAv​(J)A_{v}(J)is the subset of those neighbours that may still be occupied.
The graphsHvH_{v}are disjoint and have no edges between them. Hence the
random variablesWv:=B​(I∩V​(Hv))W_{v}:=B(I\cap V(H_{v}))are independent conditional onJJ, andB​(I)​=𝑑​B​(J)+∑v∈SWvconditional on​J.B(I)\overset{d}{=}B(J)+\sum_{v\in S}W_{v}\qquad\text{conditional on }J.

Letεv:=1\varepsilon_{v}:=1forv∈Lv\in Landεv:=−1\varepsilon_{v}:=-1forv∈Rv\in R. Ifλv\lambda_{v}andλv¯\lambda_{\bar{v}}denote respectively
the activities on the side containingvvand on the opposite side, thenZv​(J)=λv+(1+λv¯)mv,ℙ​(Wv=0∣J)=1Zv​(J),ℙ​(Wv=εv∣J)=λvZv​(J).Z_{v}(J)=\lambda_{v}+(1+\lambda_{\bar{v}})^{m_{v}},\qquad\mathbb{P}(W_{v}=0\mid J)=\frac{1}{Z_{v}(J)},\qquad\mathbb{P}(W_{v}=\varepsilon_{v}\mid J)=\frac{\lambda_{v}}{Z_{v}(J)}.

Sincemv≤Δm_{v}\leq\Deltaand the activities obey the preceding uniform bounds,
there area0,a1>0a_{0},a_{1}>0, depending only onΔ,t0\Delta,t_{0}, such thatℙ​(Wv=0∣J)≥a0,ℙ​(Wv=εv∣J)≥a1​λ.\mathbb{P}(W_{v}=0\mid J)\geq a_{0},\qquad\mathbb{P}(W_{v}=\varepsilon_{v}\mid J)\geq a_{1}\lambda.

LetWv′W_{v}^{\prime}be an independent conditional copy ofWvW_{v}. SinceWv−Wv′W_{v}-W_{v}^{\prime}has a symmetric conditional distribution and1−cos⁡u≥2​u2/π21-\cos u\geq 2u^{2}/\pi^{2}for|u|≤π|u|\leq\pi, the two events in(∗)(*)give|𝔼[e−i​u​Wv∣J]|2\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}[e^{-iuW_{v}}\mid J]\right|^{2}=𝔼​[cos⁡(u​(Wv−Wv′))∣J]\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\cos(u(W_{v}-W_{v}^{\prime}))\mid J]≤1−2​ℙ​(Wv−Wv′=εv∣J)​(1−cos⁡u)\displaystyle\leq 1-2\mathbb{P}(W_{v}-W_{v}^{\prime}=\varepsilon_{v}\mid J)(1-\cos u)≤1−4​a0​a1π2​λ​u2.\displaystyle\leq 1-\frac{4a_{0}a_{1}}{\pi^{2}}\lambda u^{2}.

Indeed, the probability in the middle line is at leastℙ​(Wv=εv∣J)​ℙ​(Wv′=0∣J)\mathbb{P}(W_{v}=\varepsilon_{v}\mid J)\mathbb{P}(W_{v}^{\prime}=0\mid J).
Conditional independence, followed by|S|=ΩΔ​(n)|S|=\Omega_{\Delta}(n), now gives|𝔼[e−i​u​B​(I)∣J]|≤∏v∈S|𝔼[e−i​u​Wv∣J]|≤exp(−cλnu2).\left|\mathbb{E}[e^{-iuB(I)}\mid J]\right|\leq\prod_{v\in S}\left|\mathbb{E}[e^{-iuW_{v}}\mid J]\right|\leq\exp(-c\lambda nu^{2}).

Averaging overJJproves (2.30), and hence

algorithm outputs the empty independent set. Every successful output has lawμG,λbal\mu_{G,\lambda}^{\mathrm{bal}}, sinceet~​B​(I)=1e^{\widetilde{t}B(I)}=1onB​(I)=0B(I)=0. Thus the exact-proposal version is within total variation distanceϵ/5\epsilon/5ofμG,λbal\mu_{G,\lambda}^{\mathrm{bal}}.

The actual implementation makes at mostJ​N+MJN+M𝖳𝗂𝗅𝗍𝖾𝖽𝖲𝖺𝗆𝗉𝗅𝖾𝗋\mathsf{TiltedSampler}calls. By the same coupling argument as in
(2.19) and a union bound, its output law is within(J​N+M)​τ≤ϵ/10(JN+M)\tau\leq\epsilon/10in total variation distance of that of the
exact-proposal version.

The total error is at mostϵ/5+ϵ/10=3​ϵ/10≤ϵ\epsilon/5+\epsilon/10=3\epsilon/10\leq\epsilon. All searched
tilts lie in[−t0,t0][-t_{0},t_{0}], where Algorithm3runs in
polynomial time, and all parameters are polynomial innnand1/ϵ1/\epsilon.
∎

## 3FPTAS for the balanced partition function

This section establishes the FPTAS assertion below the uniqueness threshold in
Theorem1.2. In fact, we prove the stronger assertion for
the class𝒢~Δ,Δγ\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\Delta,\Delta}^{\gamma}defined in
(2.1).

## Proposition 3.1.

FixΔ≥3\Delta\geq 3,0<λ<λc​(Δ)0<\lambda<\lambda_{c}(\Delta), andγ≥1\gamma\geq 1. Then there is an
FPTAS forZGbal​(λ)Z_{G}^{\mathrm{bal}}(\lambda)on inputsG∈𝒢~Δ,ΔγG\in\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\Delta,\Delta}^{\gamma}.

Throughout this section, fixγ≥1\gamma\geq 1andG=(L⊔R,E)∈𝒢~Δ,ΔγG=(L\sqcup R,E)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\Delta,\Delta}^{\gamma}and writen:=|V​(G)|=|L|+|R|n:=|V(G)|=|L|+|R|. We also fix0<λ<λc​(Δ)0<\lambda<\lambda_{c}(\Delta). As in
Section2, after possibly exchanging the two sides of
the bipartition, we assume that|L|≥|R||L|\geq|R|. Consequently,
(2.2) holds.

Lett0=t0​(Δ,λ,γ)>0t_{0}=t_{0}(\Delta,\lambda,\gamma)>0be the compact-tilt constant from
Theorem2.24. By
(2.2), the centering tiltt∗t^{*}, characterized
by𝔼μλ,t∗⁡B​(I)=0\operatorname{\mathbbm{E}}_{\mu_{\lambda,t^{*}}}B(I)=0, lies in the compact interval[−t0,t0][-t_{0},t_{0}]. All tilts supplied to the subroutines below will lie in this
interval.

A proof overview is given next. The key basic identity is that, for every tilttt,ZGbal​(λ)=ZG​(λ;t)​Prμλ,t⁡{B​(I)=0}.Z_{G}^{\mathrm{bal}}(\lambda)=Z_{G}(\lambda;t)\,\Pr_{\mu_{\lambda,t}}\{B(I)=0\}.(3.1)

Fix a constantA>0A>0, depending only onΔ\Deltaandλ\lambda.
Based on (3.1), an FPTAS forZGbal​(λ)Z_{G}^{\mathrm{bal}}(\lambda)requires the following three tasks, which we want to implement in time polynomial innnand1/ϵ1/\epsilonand with constants depending only onΔ,λ,γ,A\Delta,\lambda,\gamma,A. Recall the notationm​(t):=𝔼μλ,t⁡B​(I).m(t):=\operatorname{\mathbbm{E}}_{\mu_{\lambda,t}}B(I).
- 1.

Find a deterministic tiltt~∈[−t0,t0]\widetilde{t}\in[-t_{0},t_{0}]such that|m​(t~)|≤A|m(\widetilde{t})|\leq A.
- 2.

Givent~\widetilde{t}, approximateZG​(λ;t~)Z_{G}(\lambda;\widetilde{t})to relative error1±O​(ϵ)1\pm O(\epsilon).
- 3.

Givent~\widetilde{t}, approximatePrμλ,t~⁡{B​(I)=0}\Pr_{\mu_{\lambda,\widetilde{t}}}\{B(I)=0\}to relative error1±O​(ϵ)1\pm O(\epsilon).

Splitting the error budget among the last two multiplicative approximations
and using (3.1) then gives anϵ\epsilon-relative approximation toZGbal​(λ)Z_{G}^{\mathrm{bal}}(\lambda).
The first two steps are standard consequences of the compact-tilt SAW-tree
approximation algorithms. The last step involves "algorithmizing" the local CLT
proof, in the spirit of[24].

## 3.1Finding a deterministic tilt and approximatingZGZ_{G}

We first show that a deterministic tilt search can replace
the empirical search used in the sampling algorithm.

## Lemma 3.2.

Suppose (2.2) holds, and letA>0A>0. There is a deterministic algorithm which returns a tiltt~∈[−t0,t0]\widetilde{t}\in[-t_{0},t_{0}]satisfying|m​(t~)|≤A|m(\widetilde{t})|\leq A

in time polynomial innn, possibly depending on the constantsΔ,λ,γ,A\Delta,\lambda,\gamma,A.

## Proof.

Setη:=A/10\eta:=A/10. At each queried tiltr∈[−t0,t0]r\in[-t_{0},t_{0}], use
Algorithm2to approximate every occupation marginal to
additive errorη/n\eta/n. Equation (2.3) then gives
an estimatem^​(r)\widehat{m}(r)with|m^​(r)−m​(r)|≤η|\widehat{m}(r)-m(r)|\leq\eta.

Run bisection on[−t0,t0][-t_{0},t_{0}]as in the proof of
Proposition2.1. If|m^​(r)|<2​η|\widehat{m}(r)|<2\eta, returnrr, since then|m​(r)|<3​η≤A|m(r)|<3\eta\leq A. Otherwisem^​(r)\widehat{m}(r)andm​(r)m(r)have the same
sign, so the retained interval containst∗t^{*}. If no early stop occurs,
continue until its width is at mostA/(4​Cvar​n)A/(4C_{\mathrm{var}}n), where𝖵𝖺𝗋μλ,s⁡(B​(I))≤Cvar​n\operatorname{\mathsf{Var}}_{\mu_{\lambda,s}}(B(I))\leq C_{\mathrm{var}}non[−t0,t0][-t_{0},t_{0}]by Lemma2.19. Its midpointt~\widetilde{t}then satisfies, bym′​(s)=𝖵𝖺𝗋μλ,s⁡(B​(I))m^{\prime}(s)=\operatorname{\mathsf{Var}}_{\mu_{\lambda,s}}(B(I)),|m​(t~)|≤Cvar​n​|t~−t∗|≤A/8≤A.|m(\widetilde{t})|\leq C_{\mathrm{var}}n|\widetilde{t}-t^{*}|\leq A/8\leq A.

The search makesO​(log⁡n)O(\log n)bisection steps, each usingnnmarginal oracle calls, and is therefore polynomial-time.
∎

## Lemma 3.3.

Suppose (2.2) holds. There is a
deterministic algorithm which, given a tiltt∈[−t0,t0]t\in[-t_{0},t_{0}]andϵ∈(0,1)\epsilon\in(0,1), outputs anϵ\epsilon-relative approximation toZG​(λ;t)Z_{G}(\lambda;t)in timepoly​(n,1/ϵ)\mathrm{poly}(n,1/\epsilon), with constants depending onΔ,λ,γ\Delta,\lambda,\gamma.

## Proof.

This is the standard hard-core self-reduction[38, Section 5], also used for
Algorithm3. For an orderingv1,…,vnv_{1},\ldots,v_{n}, letpip_{i}be the occupation probability ofviv_{i}in the residual graphG−{v1,…,vi−1}G-\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{i-1}\}, with its inherited activities. ThenZG​(λ;t)=∏i(1−pi)−1.Z_{G}(\lambda;t)=\prod_{i}(1-p_{i})^{-1}.

These activities remain in the compact tilt window, so the SAW-tree oracle
approximates eachpip_{i}to additive accuracyO​(ϵ/n)O(\epsilon/n), while1−pi1-p_{i}is uniformly bounded away from zero. The product above then gives
the claimed relative approximation.
∎

## 3.2Algorithmic local CLT for compact tilts

The subroutine for estimating the factorPrμλ,t⁡{B​(I)=0}\Pr_{\mu_{\lambda,t}}\{B(I)=0\}in (3.1) is based on an algorithmic version of the compact-tilt
local CLT, Theorem2.17.

## Proposition 3.4.

Suppose (2.2) holds, and letA>0A>0. There is a deterministic algorithm which, given a tiltt∈[−t0,t0]t\in[-t_{0},t_{0}]satisfying|m​(t)|≤A|m(t)|\leq A, andϵ∈(0,1)\epsilon\in(0,1), outputs anϵ\epsilon-relative approximation toPrμλ,t⁡{B​(I)=0}\Pr_{\mu_{\lambda,t}}\{B(I)=0\}

in timepoly​(n,1/ϵ)\mathrm{poly}(n,1/\epsilon), with constants depending onΔ,λ,γ,A\Delta,\lambda,\gamma,A.

Our proof strategy for Proposition3.4is given next. Recall the tilted complex partition function (2.20): forζ∈ℂ\zeta\in\mathbb{C}ZG​(λ;t,ζ)=∑I∈ℐGλ|I|​et​B​(I)​ζB​(I).Z_{G}(\lambda;t,\zeta)=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{G}}\lambda^{|I|}e^{tB(I)}\zeta^{B(I)}.

Fourier inversion on the latticeℤ\mathbb{Z}, followed by the change of
variablesθ=n​u\theta=\sqrt{n}\,u, givesPrμλ,t⁡{B​(I)=0}=12​π​n​∫−π​nπ​nZG​(λ;t,ei​θ/n)ZG​(λ;t)​𝑑θ.\Pr_{\mu_{\lambda,t}}\{B(I)=0\}=\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{n}}\int_{-\pi\sqrt{n}}^{\pi\sqrt{n}}\frac{Z_{G}(\lambda;t,e^{i\theta/\sqrt{n}})}{Z_{G}(\lambda;t)}\,d\theta.(3.2)

The algorithmic tasks are:
- •

Approximate the partition function ratioZG​(λ;t,ζ)/ZG​(λ;t,1)Z_{G}(\lambda;t,\zeta)/Z_{G}(\lambda;t,1)forζ\zetanear11. This will be done using the complex SAW-tree which gives approximations to the
occupation ratios.
- •

Approximate the Fourier integral separately for low and high frequencies. LetT=O​(log⁡(1/ϵ))T=O(\sqrt{\log(1/\epsilon)}). By
taking then\sqrt{n}-scale, the quantityei​θ/ne^{i\theta/\sqrt{n}}is close to11on|θ|≤T|\theta|\leq T, so the integrand is in the range of the ratio
approximation from the first bullet. We approximate this part of the integral by
evaluating the ratio at finitely many evenly spaced points. For|θ|>T|\theta|>T,
we reuse the compact-tilt high-frequency estimate from the local CLT proof,
Lemma2.23, to show that this part of the
integral is negligible.

This approach is inspired by that in[24]. A difference is that here, we estimate the partition function ratio in (3.2) directly. The route taken in[24]via
zero-freeness and Barvinok[1]and Patel–Regts[33]to approximate the characteristic function ofB​(I)B(I)would likely also work. However, our approach leverages the complex SAW-tree machinery already developed in Section2.4.

Our next result gives the complex ratio oracle needed for the low-frequency
partition function approximation. The proof builds on the zero-freeness argument in
Section2.4, especially
Lemma2.16and the proof of
Proposition2.15.

Throughout the next two lemmas, for fixedttandζ\zeta, an induced
subgraphH⊆GH\subseteq G, and a vertexu∈V​(H)u\in V(H), writeRH,u:=λ†(u)​(ζ)​ZH∖NH​[u]​(λ;t,ζ)ZH−u​(λ;t,ζ).R_{H,u}:=\frac{\lambda_{\dagger(u)}(\zeta)\,Z_{H\setminus N_{H}[u]}(\lambda;t,\zeta)}{Z_{H-u}(\lambda;t,\zeta)}.

This is defined whenever the denominator is nonzero. To emphasize
the dependence onζ\zeta, we may writeRH,u​(ζ)R_{H,u}(\zeta). An elementary
decomposition of independent sets according to whetheruuis absent or
present givesZH​(λ;t,ζ)=ZH−u​(λ;t,ζ)​(1+RH,u).Z_{H}(\lambda;t,\zeta)=Z_{H-u}(\lambda;t,\zeta)\bigl(1+R_{H,u}\bigr).(3.3)
